Budapest exhibition explores Van Gogh and Gauguin's artistic excursion to Montpellier

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The portico of Budapest’s Museum of Fine Art

Adventures with Van Gogh is a weekly blog by Martin Bailey, The Art Newspaper's long-standing correspondent and expert on the Dutch painter. Published on Fridays, stories range from newsy items about this most intriguing artist, to scholarly pieces based on meticulous investigations and discoveries.

Explore all of Martin’s adventures with Van Gogh here.

After Paul Gauguin arrived in Arles in October 1888 to stay with his friend Van Gogh, the two artists would go out to paint together and then socialise and discuss art in the evenings over a glass of wine. Their very different personalities, however, soon led to tensions. Living together in the confines of the Yellow House and sharing a small room as a studio, they began to row. As winter approached, and the weather made it impossible to work outside, the situation only worsened.

In mid-December, in an attempt to clear the air, Gauguin suggested that they should make a trip to Montpellier, 70 km west of Arles. Their goal was to visit the Musée Fabre, renowned for its fine collection of paintings.

The artistic result of this trip forms a central part of a major exhibition on Gauguin which opened this week at Budapest’s Museum of Fine Arts. Bonjour Monsieur Gauguin: The Circles of Post-Impressionists and Symbolists (until 28 February 2027) provides a wide-ranging exploration of the art of Gauguin and his associates. The show’s title comes from a key painting by the artist inspired by the Montpellier visit, Bonjour Monsieur Gauguin (late 1889).

Paul Gauguin’s Bonjour Monsieur Gauguin (late 1889)

Hammer Museum, Los Angeles (photograph The Art Newspaper)

Van Gogh and Gauguin travelled from Arles to Montpellier by train, which took around four hours. Although it has long been assumed that they made a day trip on either 16 or 17 December 1888, it is arguably more likely that they spent the night there and stayed for both days. The Musée Fabre had over 800 paintings on display, so they would surely have wanted time to savour the works. Many of the finest pictures were French, given by Alfred Bruyas, a local collector and patron of Gustave Courbet.

Gustave Courbet’s Bonjour Monsieur Courbet (1854)

For Gauguin, the highlight of the museum was Courbet’s Bonjour Monsieur Courbet (1854). This landscape composition includes the figure of Courbet himself, laden with his equipment and holding a walking pole. He is greeting Bruyas, who is accompanied by a bowing servant.

Gustave Courbet’s Bonjour Monsieur Courbet and Gauguin’s Bonjour Monsieur Gauguin in the Budapest exhibition

Gauguin, who was impressed with the Courbet, painted a work in response, a year later, in the Brittany fishing village of Le Pouldu. The main figure in this work is Gauguin himself, facing a Breton woman on the other side of a gate.

Superficially, the Gauguin painting bears little resemblance to the Courbet. Stylistically, Courbet’s smoothly painted picture contrasts with Gauguin’s rough brushwork. The compositions appear to have little in common, other than showing an artist encountering other people—and being greeted with a “good day” (and, incidentally, both scenes also include a dog).

The moods seem very different. Courbet appears thrilled to greet his patron and friend. Gauguin’s encounter with the Breton woman, however, is fleeting, as they happen to pass each other at a gate. The Budapest exhibition’s lead curator, Judit Geskó, sees the Gauguin picture as representing “the artist as a solitary wanderer”.

But despite the differences, Gauguin’s decision to entitle his work as he did shows that he was directly acknowledging the source of his inspiration, the Courbet. Gauguin’s title is actually inscribed on the painting, in the lower-left corner.

Gauguin made two versions of his composition: the one on loan to Budapest from the Hammer Museum in Los Angeles and the other (which was unavailable for borrowing) at the National Gallery in Prague. The Hammer picture, which was the original, was created to display on the dining room door in the small inn where he was staying in Le Pouldu.

As for Van Gogh, the Dutchman was particularly struck in the Musée Fabre by the portraits by Courbet. These included a self-portrait, The Man with the Pipe (around 1849), which may well have inspired Van Gogh one month later to paint his Self-Portrait with Bandaged Ear and Pipe (January 1889).

Bruyas was depicted by a range of artists and these portraits were also much admired by Van Gogh in the Montpellier museum. Vincent described Bruyas’ appearance to his brother Theo as “a gentleman with a beard, red hair, who looks damnably like you or me”.

Postcard of Musée Fabre, Montpellier (1905)

Both Gauguin and Van Gogh were great admirers of Courbet, who was very well represented in the Musée Fabre. Although they agreed about Courbet, their tastes for other artists were often rather different. Vincent wrote to Theo that their artistic discussions in Montpellier had been “excessively electric”. He explained: “We sometimes emerge from it with tired minds, like an electric battery after it’s run down.”

Despite these animated discussions, the inspirational visit seemed a success. Vincent reported back to Theo the day after their return that in the museum they had been “right in the midst of magic”. He added: “Gauguin said to me this morning, when I asked him how he felt: ‘that he could feel his old self coming back’, which gave me great pleasure.”

Sadly, the reconciliation did not last long: just four days later Van Gogh severely mutilated his ear. Although the trigger was probably news he had just received that Theo had become engaged (which Vincent feared would threaten his brother’s support), tensions with Gauguin were also a contributing factor.

Budapest’s Gauguin exhibition is ambitious, with 80 works by the French master and 70 by other artists. Although financial valuations for exhibitions are rarely revealed, The Art Newspaper can report that its total insurance value is €1.5bn.

Van Gogh’s Field with Irises near Arles (May 1888)

Van Gogh Museum, Amsterdam (Vincent van Gogh Foundation)

The show includes three works by Van Gogh: the landscape painting Field with Irises near Arles (May 1888, Van Gogh Museum, Amsterdam) and two rarely-displayed drawings from the collection of the Budapest museum, The Parsonage Garden at Nuenen in Winter (March 1884) and Haystacks (July 1888).

Van Gogh’s The Parsonage Garden at Nuenen in Winter (March 1884) and Haystacks (July 1888)

The Budapest exhibition has two Gauguin paintings dating from his stay with Van Gogh in Arles: the landscape Les Alyscamps (October 1888, Musée d’Orsay, Paris) and the portrait Madame Roulin (November-December 1888, Saint Louis Art Museum). Both of these subjects were also tackled by Van Gogh.

Gauguin’s Les Alyscamps (October 1888) and Madame Roulin (November-December 1888)

Musée d’Orsay, Paris and Saint Louis Art Museum

Among the other Gauguin highlights in the show is Sunflowers on an Armchair (1901, Bührle Collection, on loan to the Kunsthaus Zurich). This is one of four still-life paintings inspired by Van Gogh’s favourite motif—sunflowers. Gauguin painted these in the Marquesas Islands (north of Tahiti), with flowers grown from seeds he was sent by a friend from Paris.

Gauguin’s Sunflowers on an Armchair (1901)

Bührle Collection, on loan to the Kunsthaus Zurich

The museum estimates that the Gauguin exhibition could see up to 350,000 visitors, a huge number for a medium-sized European capital. However, even this won’t match Geskó's Van Gogh show in 2006, which attracted 486,000 people to the museum to see the Dutch painter's works.

Martin Bailey is a leading Van Gogh specialist and special correspondent for The Art Newspaper. He has curated exhibitions at the Barbican Art Gallery, Compton Verney/National Gallery of Scotland and Tate Britain.

Martin has written a number of bestselling books on Van Gogh’s years in France: The Sunflowers Are Mine: The Story of Van Gogh's Masterpiece (Frances Lincoln 2013, UK and US), Studio of the South: Van Gogh in Provence (Frances Lincoln 2016, UK and US), Starry Night: Van Gogh at the Asylum (White Lion Publishing 2018, UK and US) and Van Gogh’s Finale: Auvers and the Artist’s Rise to Fame (Frances Lincoln 2021, UK and US). The Sunflowers are Mine (2024, UK and US) and Van Gogh’s Finale (2024, UK and US) are also now available in a more compact paperback format.

His other recent books include Living with Vincent van Gogh: The Homes & Landscapes that shaped the Artist (White Lion Publishing 2019, UK and US), which provides an overview of the artist’s life. The Illustrated Provence Letters of Van Gogh has been reissued (Batsford 2021, UK and US). My Friend Van Gogh/Emile Bernard provides the first English translation of Bernard’s writings on Van Gogh (David Zwirner Books 2023, UKand US).

To contact Martin Bailey, please email vangogh@theartnewspaper.com

Please note that he does not undertake authentications.

Ratchet & Clank: Ranger Rumble Shutting Down In 2027

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Ratchet & Clank: Ranger Rumble, a new mobile hero shooter spin-off, launched globally in October, but it's already being shut down.

"We've made the difficult decision to cease development," developer Oh BiBi announced. "The final planned update will be rolling out November 6th, 2026, and the app will be removed from stores on November 21st, 2026. Players who already have the game will be able to continue playing until May 1st, 2027, when the game’s servers will permanently shut down."

Oh BiBi explained that once the servers have been taken offline, "the game will no longer load" and all player data will be wiped from the servers. In the meantime, in-app purchases have been disabled, and the Super Pass is now free.

Ranger Rumble is the first new entry in the Ratchet & Clank series since Rift Apart in 2021, set in Captain Qwark's "totally safe simulation." Each match is essentially a training mission, with 12 unique heroes spanning from Zed to Tempest and Lump, with new Lombax characters like Sprocket, Chip, Widget, and Mopz thrown into the mix. Bizarrely, however, Ratchet was an event exclusive character.

At this point, us Ratchet fans will take what we can get.

The game also featured a few different modes to choose from, including a battle royale and Rocket League-esque match up:

You might be surprised to hear that there was a free-to-play Ratchet & Clank hero shooter on mobile, but it was pretty well-received among those who did play it. The game currently holds a 4.5 star rating on Google Play, but with just 100,00+ downloads, only a fraction of fans ever got around to trying it.

The original Ratchet & Clank is finally joining the PS Plus classics catalogue later this year.

How to get Sweet Dreams Sprite in Fortnite Treasure Hunters

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Treasure Hunters mini-season in Fortnite comes with its own array of unique Sprites you can find and collect around the island. This season is shorter than usual, so you’ll have to act fast to find them all. Perhaps the trickiest one to get is the Sweet Dreams Sprite.

Sprite hunting is generally a pretty tough process, especially for Mythic variants like this one. If you’re having trouble tracking it down, here’s how to get the Sweet Dreams Sprite in Fortnite.

The Sweet Dreams Sprite is a random loot drop item, which means you need to go looting around the island and hope you find it. For the most part, how easy or difficult it is to collect comes down to luck and random chance, although you can focus heavily on Sprite hunting to increase your odds.

Sprites can sometimes be found wandering around the island, but rare ones like this one are very uncommon to spot this way. Instead, you’ll want to focus on using Sprite Wanted Posters to dig up and open Sprite Chests and opening Vaults to find Sprite Chests. These two methods are the overall best ways to add Sprites to your collection.

Read what’s behind the latest news in gaming (with a bit of snark) by subscribing to the Destructoid Checkpoint newsletter!

If you’re determined to add the Sweet Dreams Sprite to your collection, there is one guaranteed method you can use to get it. This method takes a lot of patience, hard work, and dedication, but it does ensure you end up with this elusive Sprite in your collection.

Here’s a complete breakdown of how to guarantee you get the Sweet Dreams Sprite.

There is no guaranteed spawn location for the Sweet Dreams Sprite in Fortnite. Instead, you must loot around and hope you encounter it randomly, or work hard to use the method mentioned above to ensure you find one.

The Sweet Dreams Sprite grants you a random item each time it reaches a new level. Once it’s fully maxed out at level five, it grants you an array of Mythic loot. The value of the loot it provides increases with each level.

Duo Completes Baffin Island Circuit By Skiing, Hiking, and Packrafting

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Caleb McDaniels, 25, and Henry Penfold, 22, have completed their impressive expedition around Baffin Island. The Americans combined skiing, hiking, and packrafting to almost circle the world’s fifth-largest island. McDaniels told ExplorersWeb they did about 20% skiing and the rest was evenly split between backpacking and packrafting.

The pair began and ended in Iqaluit, Nunavut’s capital. The final section of their trip was also the longest, a 1,130km stretch with no villages after Igloolik to resupply. They made light work of it, knocking it out in 40 days and bringing their total distance to 3,262km over 145 days, from their late May start to their return to Iqaluit in mid-October. By then, the oncoming Arctic winter gave them few hours of light to work with.

The pair’s completed route around Baffin Island. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

With no resupply points, the pair left Igloolik with 82kg of food. They hoped to do it in seven weeks but brought even more food, because they weren’t sure how long that giant giant section would actually take, explained Caleb McDaniels.

“With no way to carry that much weight in our packs, we loaded up our packrafts and paddled the first 400 miles [644km]. Then, for the final 300 miles [483km], we heaved on our packs to walk overland,” they explained.

The paddling portion went quickly, aided by some ingenuity. “Much of the time, we were able to sail using our trekking poles as masts and panels cut out of an old tent for sails. Speeding along at over five mph, we did some incredible downwinding, spray flying from our bows.”

Using makeshift sails. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

They also effectively halved the 480km trek by packrafting over two large lakes, including Nettilling, the world’s largest lake on an island. Yet there was no way to ease the final 160km slog into Iqaluit.

Postholing through early winter snow back to Iqaluit. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

“It was more of a postholing session — we slowly picked our way across snow-covered boulder fields and partly frozen marshlands. Constantly breaking through thin ice over the tundra marshes, we walked in our drysuits to keep our legs and feet dry. Each morning required half an hour of chipping ice and melting out our shoes, which invariably froze solid,” they said.

McDaniels and Penfold had planned a roughly circular route around the island, but “we weren’t trying to circumnavigate Baffin,” McDaniels told ExplorersWeb. Two previous parties had circumnavigated the island by dogteam, but McDaniels and Penfold’s route was driven by aesthetic considerations rather than to satisfy a catchy trip definition.

Slogging overland. Photo: Caleb McDaniels

They chose their route after looking at satellite images and enjoyed both spectacular scenery and some special wildlife sightings, including at least one that was a little too close for comfort.

“One morning, as I went to fill our water bottles, I stumbled upon a sleeping polar bear hidden behind a low rise. Only a few yards away, it lifted its massive white head and glared at me for a moment before returning to its nap in the sun,” McDaniels wrote. “I carefully retreated behind the rise and yelled over to Henry to get the shotgun and flares out just in case. We quickly packed our boats and got on the water.”

In Foxe Basin, they almost collided with a sleeping walrus, and on Nettilling Lake, they met one of the resident seals. “He was immensely curious and not at all afraid. He swam around our boats for half an hour, playing with our stern lines and swimming back and forth between us. At one point, I put my hand into the water, and he brushed it with his nose!”

Martin Walsh is a writer and editor for ExplorersWeb.

Martin spent most of the last 20 years backpacking the world on a shoestring budget. Whether it was hitchhiking through Syria, getting strangled in Kyrgyzstan, touring Cambodia’s medical facilities with an exceedingly painful giant venomous centipede bite, chewing khat in Ethiopia, or narrowly avoiding various toilet-related accidents in rural China, so far, Martin has just about survived his decision-making.

Based in Da Lat, Vietnam, Martin can be found in the jungle trying to avoid leeches while chasing monkeys.

ExplorersWeb is part of AllGear Digital's portfolio of media brands.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

We've been early adopters of Rust since 2018 and a lot of our infra, voice tech, and even some of the current client is already Rust, the holy grail was getting the whole client there. That means Electron goes away, and so does React Native, mobile keeps native UI but shares app… https://t.co/2CgWyOzaq0 pic.twitter.com/IdOGd4nUglNovember 4, 2026

Open up Task Manager and check how much memory Discord is currently hogging on your PC. If it's anything like mine, it's probably north of 500 MB. Alas, things should change soon, as Discord is moving the client completely over to a new programming language. It might not sound like that big of a deal, but Discord's CTO, Stanislav Vishnevskiy, shows it running on as little as 290 MB of memory.

Discord is switching from Electron to Rust. Electron is a software framework, a building block that allows JavaScript apps (among others) to run standalone on desktops. Built on Chromium, the underlying model behind Google Chrome, there is an overhead here that swapping to Rust aims to fix.

Vishnevskiy says, "We've been early adopters of Rust since 2018 and a lot of our infra, voice tech, and even some of the current client is already Rust, the holy grail was getting the whole client there. That means Electron goes away, and so does React Native, mobile keeps native UI but shares app code with desktop via Rust."

Though some suspected Discord would be swapping to Rust using Tauri (an open-source framework for desktop and mobile platforms), Vishnevskiy says it's actually using GPUI (a framework written in Rust), but "We have our own patches on top of it".

Vishnevskiy also says the goal is to get the program into testing with users soon, but no official date has been given. Either way, with Vishnevskiy showing off a resource demand of 290.2 MB whilst active, that's still a pretty big dip below what it runs at now. Mine is currently flitting between 500 and 600 MB, and my colleague Jacob saw it go up to 785 MB running on Windows.

The new Discord client using less RAM is just part of the story. Clicking around feels instant, I can't go back. Check out the boot time. https://t.co/YF9g9K30b9 pic.twitter.com/x7ayyWBVQJNovember 5, 2026

Reportedly, the new Discord client is also faster to use, booting much quicker. Vishnevskiy showed two laptops, one with the old client and one with the new, and the new client opens up significantly faster (six seconds versus one in my approximation).

Naturally, we are hearing this from the mouth of the company, so we'll have to get the new Discord client in our hands to see how much faster and how much more efficient it really is, but anyway, to get a little more out of your current memory will always be a boon right now.

The PC Gamer Circuit Breaker newsletter breaks down the latest PC gaming tech, and finds you the best deals on the best products

The memory crisis is continuing to drive the prices of gaming gear up, but software tweaks like this (including both Epic and Steam getting faster) are very much welcome in my eyes.

3. Best handheld gaming PC: Asus ROG Xbox Ally X

4. Best mini PC: Minisforum AtomMan G7 PT

In that time, he has covered the latest doodads, contraptions, and gismos, and loved every second of it. Hey, it’s better than writing case briefs.

You Don’t Need ‘Pervert Glasses’ to Remember Your Hike

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A woman is holding a pair of smart glasses from Ray-Ban Meta in her hand at an eyewear store. Because these glasses allow users to take photos and videos, some outdoor and indoor swimming pools are considering banning them. (Photo: Christoph Schmidt/picture alliance via Getty Images)

In the annals of uncool accessories—the pocket protector, the cell phone holster, cargo shorts—smart glasses have already earned a place of special scorn. Unlike those utilitarian pieces, the backlash against the high-tech glasses isn’t about what they look like, though their oversized temples and chunky frames do give them a distinct Spirit Halloween look. It’s what they do: Their ability to record anyone the user looks at has led governments from Norway to New York to consider restrictions and businesses like theaters and bars to ban them. After several well-publicized incidents of users deploying smart glasses to covertly film women on the street, some detractors have labeled the wearables “pervert glasses.”

Despite that stigma, Meta, the company behind both Facebook and the most popular models of smart glasses, reportedly sold 7 million pairs of them in 2025 alone, and the eyewear has become a regular sight everywhere from bike paths to restaurants. Now, Meta wants you to take them on your next hike.

In a patent application published on October 15, Meta outlined a software update for its product that would use contextual clues like audio, speech, and data from wearables to automatically edit video captured by the glasses. The first use case the company offered: Creating a highlight reel from a recording of a hike. For example, if the glasses record the sound of a waterfall or the user saying “Look at that bird,” they could tag the footage from that moment as significant; likewise if, for example, a connected smartwatch registers your heart rate spiking. While those automatic features are still pending, hiking footage shot on the Meta glasses abounds on YouTube.

For some hikers who own them, the glasses might be a fun toy. For everyone else, they’re a privacy nightmare. While the idea of a camera monitoring your words and heart rate for hours is creepy enough, it’s not Meta Glasses’ only trick: This summer, Wired discovered code for a facial recognition system in the glasses’ software, an idea Meta had previously abandoned in 2021 over ethical concerns. The New York Times reported that the proposed feature, called Name Tag, would identify people the wearer encounters and look up information about them through Muse, the company’s AI assistant.

Exactly how much of the internet that search would have scoured is an open question: The company reportedly contemplated both limiting it to a user’s friends and allowing it to search everyone with a profile on Meta platforms like Instagram, Facebook, and Threads. While Meta quietly removed the software after Wired’s report, it’s been mum on its future plans for the technology, and third-party researchers have demonstrated how easy it is to livestream footage from the glasses into existing facial recognition engines. It’s easy to imagine a worst-case scenario where a stranger passing by could learn an uncomfortable amount about your life without ever saying a word. (While a built-in LED signals when the glasses are recording, some regulators have noted that it is not easily visible in bright sunlight, and models from many lesser-known manufacturers don’t have any kind of indicator.)

The issues only multiply from there. An investigation by two Swedish newspapers found that subcontractors hired by Meta to help train its AI regularly viewed sensitive video from its smart glasses, including clips showing nudity and users going to the bathroom. And, like any data stored in the cloud, smart glasses footage is available to any law enforcement agency that can obtain a warrant. While Meta has a decent track record of protecting users’ privacy in dealing with those demands, it doesn’t have a perfect one; according to the company’s own transparency reports, it complied with 88.8 percent of formal government data requests during one six-month period last year.

One might ask: Is any of this worth it? Meta has spent tens of millions of dollars lobbying the government not to regulate it. Is letting it ride shotgun on your hike like some real-life episode of Black Mirror a good idea? Will anyone actually look at the hundreds of thousands of hours of video these glasses capture, or will it just gather dust in a data center somewhere like most of the footage people shoot on their phones? (To be fair, I’ll also note here that smart glasses have been a game-changing tool for their small population of blind and low-vision users—about 8 percent of buyers cite accessibility as their main reason, according to a report by Security.org—though they don’t seem to be the demographic Meta is serving with these proposed software updates.) But I’m not here to judge how other hikers use tech. I’m more concerned with how it affects everyone else.

The backcountry means something different to every hiker, but to a lot of us, it’s an escape—from responsibilities, from city noise, and, increasingly, from being watched. We live today in a world that’s more monitored and recorded than ever before: Between automatic license plate readers, Ring doorbells, dash cams, and commercial security systems, an average urban dweller might feasibly spend their entire day hopping from one camera’s field of view to another. The backcountry is one of the last unsurveilled environments in North America; with the glasses’ new capabilities, Meta proposes to introduce what’s rapidly becoming a face-mounted Flock camera into it.

Whether we’re talking about smart glasses, stealth camping, or hiking with off-leash dogs, discussions about how we behave in the backcountry often devolve into arguments about individual freedom and responsibility. That misses the point: There are some behaviors that affect the experience of everyone around us. The person who decides to film their hike with a wearable that monitors everything they see and hear reaps all the benefits of that decision; it’s other backpackers that I foresee bearing the cost. Nor are those costs evenly distributed: A YouGov poll found that roughly two-thirds of people interested in buying smart glasses are men, while the Security study found that women are consistently more likely to report concern about the glasses’ cameras and potential facial recognition tech.

Someone will likely read this and ask what the big deal is; anyone with a phone, after all, can already record anyone they please. But there’s a big difference between pointing a camera at another person and being able to record anyone you look at, let alone run them through face ID. The trail is where backpackers feel the most unburdened—the least obligated to perform the roles we inhabit in our everyday lives. When anyone with a pair of shades could be recording you, living freely may start to feel a lot less sustainable.

Inside Mohamed Salah's new life after his Liverpool fall-out: LEWIS STEELE visits Turkey's 30th biggest city, where the Egyptian King hikes, drinks salted yoghurt - and still gets hero worship

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published: 07:03 EDT, 6 November 2026 | Updated: 07:11 EDT, 6 November 2026

It started just minutes after touchdown in Turkey.

In a taxi journey across Istanbul's intercontinental bridge to Fenerbahce's stadium, the driver took a break from lighting up a cigarette or shouting profanities at other road users. He turned, looked me in the eye, and asked: 'You know Mohamed Salah?'

Two and a half hours after fighting through mind-blowing traffic (think your gridlocked motorway at 5pm multiplied by 100), on arrival at Liverpool's press conference, one gentleman did not even exchange pleasantries. He shoved a selfie stick in my face and asked about Salah.

And these two encounters were 650 miles away from where the Egyptian player now plies his trade. This fandom was only a starter when compared to the main course of Salahmania that greeted us upon arrival in the port city of Trabzon, to the east of Turkey on the coast of the Black Sea.

Here, his face and name were everywhere you looked. 'You come for Salah?' enquired the hotel receptionist. Upon hearing that was indeed the purpose of the visit, he upgraded the room to a superior suite overlooking the ocean. Result.

The streets of this city, which feels a world apart from the bustling metropolis of Istanbul, never mind Liverpool, are packed with antique dealers, and markets selling two-foot-long fish for less than £1 and the local delicacy of hamsi (anchovy to you and me).

Mohamed Salah can relax in Trabzon and has taken multiple hiking trips in the highlands (Source: Instagram/Mohamed Salah)

Fans have now taken to posing at the same spots Salah appeared at - such is their hero worship

Everywhere you turned, there was a nod to Salah. His name was heard in conversations as the melodic call to prayer rang out from the nearby Hagia Sophia mosque, his picture seemed to be hung from every building and his name was on the back of every shirt being sported in town.

Shops were selling mugs, scarves, plates, masks and even cigarette lighters shaped like Salah.

At the futuristic waterfront stadium known as Papara Park, Salahmania continued - with punters putting on impromptu barbecues and DJ sets from beside their cars similar to tailgates in American sports. Everyone in this city was dancing to the beat of the Egyptian King.

Fans, many in Trabzonspor-coloured jester hats with bells on, glowed about Salah. Most do not speak a word of English in this city not far from the Armenian and Georgian border. But those who could summed up the mood.

'We love him, he is going to make us champions,' said one.

Another told us: 'People across the country now support Trabzonspor and people come from other countries, people want us to win just because of Mohamed Salah.'

Trabzonspor supporters told Daily Mail Sport: 'We love him, he is going to make us champions'

Everywhere you turned there was a nod to Salah in his new club's kit

There were plenty of children in the crowd, and more women than expected. Some had come from afar to see him, others could not believe that Salah had chosen their team and hometown.

So, why Trabzon? Why did Salah choose this project in Turkey's 30th biggest city? Why not one of the three Istanbul giants – Fenerbahce, Galatasaray or Besiktas – who have won 28, 27 and 21 Turkish titles respectively, compared to Trabzonspor's seven, including only one since 1984.

The answers are obvious, according to sources. Salah, now 34, wanted a place where he could enjoy his football without the intense scrutiny. Every word and facial expression would generate days of news coverage in Liverpool or Istanbul or another one of Europe's giant cities.

On the outskirts of Trabzon, Salah can relax, hiking up to a group of boulders in Yayla. Even there, he's worshipped, though. Trabzonspor fans flock there to pose for photographs on the same rock where he sat for a photo posted on his Instagram.

Salah's family are settled, he is becoming obsessed with chess and he has started drinking the Turkish salted-yoghurt drink ayran each day.

On the pitch, there is a belief Trabzonspor can beat the big three and end Galatasaray's four-year grip on the title.

The team also stars former Liverpool midfielder Fabinho, ex-Manchester United goalkeeper Andre Onana and Jerome Boateng, once a stalwart at Bayern Munich. The manager Thomas Reis was poached from Ludogorets and has lost just once since his arrival.

Salah is settled with his family and faces less scrutiny than in Istanbul or Liverpool (Source: Instagram/Mohamed Salah)

Salah has scored 10 goals and assisted five more in his 15 games in Turkey so far

The highlight so far came on September 19, when Salah scored a hat-trick in a 4-0 thumping of Galatasaray. Trabzonspor have won seven league games in a row and will aim to go deep in the Conference League despite Thursday's undeserved 1-0 defeat to Freiburg.

Salah has scored 10 goals and assisted in five more in his 15 games in Turkey.

'Everyone wants to work with a star the size of Mohamed Salah,' striker Paul Onuachu, once of Southampton, told us after that game. 'He is a top guy and a world star.

'He adds so much to us and helps all of us, thanks to his experience and quality, and he contributes positively to the development of our team.

'I am grateful for him. It is a blessing to play with him.'

Salah did not have the rub of the green in the clash with the German side, who were Europa League finalists last year. But a trip to Papara Park underlined an answer to those wondering just why he picked Trabzon and not any of his other mega-money offers.

A man wearing Trabzonspor's claret and light blue colours shows off his support for Salah

A trip to Papara Park underlined an answer to those wondering just why he picked Trabzon

He had approaches from America, and the Saudi Arabian federation tried to sign him right until the day he jetted into Trabzon - where he was greeted by 35,000 fans with fireworks and flares. Besiktas held advanced talks with Salah's agent Ramy Abbas.

But Trabzonspor won the race and Salah is playing with a smile again, even though that face pulled a bit of a grimace when he spotted a familiar face or two from the English press pack waiting for him after the game.

His laugh faded into an expression that said: 'I thought I got rid of you lot pestering me!'

That face confirmed his reason for joining Trabzonspor: Salah is out of the spotlight – certainly while still in with a chance to win trophies and maintain a good level of football until next summer's Africa Cup of Nations.

After a public fall-out with Liverpool that saw the final year of his contract ripped up and him accuse the Anfield club of 'throwing him under the bus', it feels like Salah is feeling at home and happy again.

It's not difficult to turn down the multiple opportunities to buy Salah-branded magnets or shirts or teddies or socks or lighters as we bid farewell to Trabzon, but it is hard to escape the conclusion that the feeling of love is mutual in this football-mad city.

Former Rockstar Games tech director thinks GTA 6 could be coming to PC sooner than you'd expect to curb AI clones

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Former Rockstar Games North technical director Obbe Vermeij believes that the possibility of AI vibe coded ports of Grand Theft Auto 6 could push Rockstar to create an official PC version sooner than you'd expect.

Speaking to RockPaperShotgun as part of a wider interview, Vermeij said generative AI "really has changed the world.". He elaborated: "I think I would be concerned, if I was Rockstar, that somebody would just take the PS5 version and get AI to change it to PC, which won't be as good as [the developer's] effort, but it could still also hurt their sales. So, I wouldn't be surprised if there was an effort to get [the releases] a little bit closer together."

Vermeij emphasised that he doesn't know what Rockstar's plans are, but with his technical expertise, he does believe that generative AI tools are "probably" capable of pulling off a GTA 6 PC port.

"It will take a long time. It actually, physically takes hours and hours and hours to do these things," Vermeij said. "And it will take a bunch of tokens and everything, so it would probably cost like, tens of thousands of dollars in tokens. But like, you know, if it's GTA, I think people are gonna try it.".

We've already seen people thrust footage of Grand Theft Auto 6 into AI software over the past few months. AI slop videos have been pulling in millions of views on social media, and people have been 'improving' trailer footage with Nvidia's DLSS5 'yassification' machine (thanks Ars Technica). Grand Theft Auto 6 itself will not feature any generative AI or microtransactions at launch.

AI game clones continue to be a problem. Game developers have cancelled public demos in fear of having their game copied, and Steam owner Valve has gone out to say it's the developer's responsibility to prevent cloning by making their games more original. Even as Anthropic has introduced measures to prevent such practices, other AI software can still perform such tasks.

'Carnage' - Ex-Wolves goalkeeper Carl Ikeme offers insight into Doncaster Rovers' infamous experiment

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Former Doncaster Rovers goalkeeper Carl Ikeme has lifed the lid on his experience of the club’s now-infamous 2011/12 season ‘experiment’.

The now-retired stopper had two loan spells at Doncaster during that ill-fated Championship campaign, building experience away from his parent club Wolverhampton Wanderers.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

He was dropped into an environment far from ordinary, as Doncaster turned to high-profile figures signed to short-term deals to keep them in the second tier.

Among those recruited with the help of agent Willie McKay were former Liverpool maverick El Hadji Diouf, ex-Newcastle United defender Habib Beye and France-capped frontman Frédéric Piquionne.

“It was a bit chaotic in all honesty,” Ikeme told The Yorkshire Post. “I'm sure a lot of people have said the same.

“We had El Hadji Diouf and Pascal Chimbonda and a few other characters. I think there was talent in the squad, but I think in order to get a few of these players here, Dean Saunders kind of had to let them do what they wanted in a way, which didn't really help Doncaster.

“It was frustrating for a lot of the players that were training all the time. You probably heard some lads would just come on a Friday and train and play Saturday.

“It all seemed okay at the time if you're looking maybe from an outside point of view, but when you're in it yourself, it's a bit of a p**s take.”

Ikeme only dropped in at Doncaster for two short-term loans but committed to relocating to the area from his Midlands home.

“It was frustrating because I was living up there and obviously you had lads who would come down on a Friday,” the now-40-year-old said. “I'm staying up Doncaster all week, which is fine because that's my job.

“But it just gets frustrating I think and you had some good lads there as well, but it was hard work in all honesty.”

The so-called experiment was unequivocally a failure and Doncaster tumbled into League One at the end of the season.

Ikeme was back at Wolves by the time relegation was confirmed and did not leave Molineux again, seeing out his career with the club before retiring in 2018.

“It makes you know what you're not about, and what you don't want,” he said. “But at the same time I met some great people at Doncaster.

“Dave ‘Mud’ Richards, the kitman, great guy. Mal Purchase, who went everywhere with Dean, another great character man, really good guy. The goalkeeper coach, there were a lot of good people there behind the scenes, but it was just carnage and it wasn't how a team should really be run.

“I understand it to a degree, but football is a tough game and sometimes it doesn't really matter if you've got players that are supposed to be above the level, if they don't really care then it's going to be difficult.

“Obviously the Championship's outrageously hard anyway, and if everyone's not pulling in the same direction it becomes really difficult, and that was kind of a moment where it was really difficult.

“I liked to play games, which was good. The club, really good club as well. I really had a lot of time for Doncaster and everyone up there, it was just the situation at the time that wasn't ideal.”

Ikeme spoke The Yorkshire Post on behalf of Midnite. Midnite, the Principal Partner of Wolverhampton Wanderers for the 2026/27 season, are providing luxury coach travel on the ‘Midnite Express’ for 100 Wolverhampton Wanderers supporters, as they set off on a 130-mile-round trip to watch their beloved Wolves take on the league’s glamour club, Wrexham, this Saturday, November 7.

Fans will be joined on the Express by Wolves club legends, Steve Bull, Carl Ikeme and Matt Jarvis and treated to club-centric quizzes and live Q&As, with exclusive prizes up for grabs.

My AT Thru-Hike: By the Numbers

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Hey folks, back at it with another post here.  I’ve been meaning to write a recap of my hike pretty much sense I finished hiking the trail back in August, but alas, I am only getting to it now. Now that I am settled firmly into post-trail life, back home and working again, I took the chance to analyze my hike a bit. Compiled below are some interesting or funny insights (at least to me) of my thru-hike of the Appalachian Trail.  Hopefully you will find this insightful if you are planning your own AT thru-hike or at least entertaining…

Here is a complete breakdown of the costs that I incurred while on my At thru-hike.  While I don’t think I was egregious in my spending, I didn’t exactly skimp either.  I could have easily shaved off some expenses, especially in the food department.  However, after long hiking days, I simply enjoyed the comforts provided by cold beverages and warm food!

Right out of the pouch, just like God intended!

Along the trail I saw and experienced much, including wildlife!  The highlight here were the Fishers!  I only got a quick glimpse of both of them, as they are quite elusive creatures, but I will take what I can get!  And while I saw quite a few deer all along the trail, the deer in Shenandoah and the Smokies were shameless!  Often coming right up to my tent at night or in the morning, including one that didn’t even flinch when I popped my head out and was close enough I could have pet it!

This was surprisingly close to Tinker Cliffs in the Virginia Triple Crown area!

For sleeping along the trail I definitely preferred my tent over shelters, and spent a good deal of the trail actively avoiding shelters!  In Maine I softened this stance quite a bit for a few reasons.  The first being that Maine was the rainiest state of my hike, so there was some shelter hopping from one to another in the attempt to keep some of my gear and myself dry.  The second being I was exhausted a lot towards the end and saving a little bit of time and energy by sleeping in the shelter was worth it.  And finally, towards the end I just couldn’t bother to care about mice running around me at night that much.  Plus, the rest of the Board (Shooter and Birdie) was doing it!

First night on trail at Hidden Pond during the March Megastorm. I met friends that I would keep through the end of the trail. Especially Shooter, Queso, Zipper, and Beer Run!

Here are some fun and random stats from my hike.  These add some color to the story of my hike. Like falling 10ft off a dam, cutting your leg open, and needing to get stitches!

Day 11 – Pulling the Ripcord

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

After a long night of contemplation and hard choices, I decided to get off the trail.  I considered continuing to Tan Hill and its famous pub.  However, I believe that Tan Hill is even more remote than Keld.  If I arrived and decided that I needed medical assistance, it would be nearly impossible to arrange.  Additionally, the pub at Tan Hill was completely booked, so I would have to rely on camping on the grounds around the pub.

I would need to find my way to the nearest town and make a plan from there.  Somehow, I still need to get to Kirk Yetholm by my previously-planned arrival date.  I mailed my extra gear to the Border Hotel and I have a two-night reservation.  Graeme and Rachel told me that they will be finishing the Way on that same day.  If I keep to my schedule, I could meet up with them at the end of the Pennine and celebrate their accomplishment.

Two days after that, I need to be in Edinburgh for my scheduled flight home.

After packing up my camp, I walked into the Keld proper.  It was even smaller and more remote than I realized.  Sitting at the Keld Green Café, sipping a cappuccino, I discovered that the nearest town was Kirkby Stephan, about 10 miles away.  There are no buses in Keld.  I’d be looking at a 4 hour walk at best.

I tried using the Uber app, but after an hour of searching, the app literally apologized and gave up.  I tried a taxi app as well and received the same result.  No one was willing to make the drive from Kirkby Stephen.  I talked to the café owner; he suggested I try hitchhiking.

As I sat contemplating my options, I met Richard and Jackie, two people who were walking England’s Coast-to-Coast trail.  That trail intersects the Pennine Way at Keld.  Jackie was not taking my situation lightly.  She got on her phone and started calling taxi companies in Kirkby Stephen.  Before long, she had arranged to have a taxi make the long haul to Keld and back.  It was pricey, but at this point, I was glad to pay it.

It was almost an hour before the cab showed up.  While I waited, Jackie insisted on buying me another cappuccino.  When the driver pulled up, I thanked her profusely, declared to everyone at the café that she was a living angel, and ran for the cab.

On the drive down to Kirkby Stephan, I could see why drivers were reluctant to pick up fares from Keld.  The country road is long, narrow, and winding.  I’m not prone to motion sickness, but this road really pushed my tolerance.  After 40 minutes, we pulled up in front of the Pennine Inn.  I tipped the driver generously and went inside.  The room was lovely, and the staff, even lovelier.  Realizing that I was injured, they went out of their way to help me.  They were absolutely incredible.

Now came the task of figuring out how I was going to get from this inn to Kirk Yetholm in Scotland, nearly 120 miles away.  After hours on my phone, planning, purchasing train tickets, and making hotel reservations, I came up with the following itinerary:

I made all my reservations at hotels and inns within walking distance of the nearest train station.  Many of them were across the street from the station.

The next issue was timing the day of arrival in Kirk Yetholm.  My impression was that this tiny village would not offer a lot of entertainment or distraction.  Getting there too early was not going to be much fun.  Therefore, I decided that I would take a day or two at the end of each travel leg on my schedule.  Be a tourist!  See some of the sites along my route and do my best to enjoy my time in the UK.

And that’s what I did.  I visited towns and cities, toured castles, ate good food, touched the North Sea, and generally just wandered around admiring whatever crossed my path.  Unfortunately, I was still limping, even with the ankle brace, and had to limit my walking distance.  There were occasional bouts of melancholy over my failed attempt at the Pennine.  I felt good about walking nearly 120 miles of the Way, certainly the longest distance I had ever experienced by a very wide margin.  Mostly, I just tried to enjoy my travels.

Kidnapped Teenager Rescued After Reaching Out For Help In Among Us

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A 17-year-old Utah teenager was rescued by authorities in Oregon last month after being held captive for almost 18 months by 39-year-old Adam Hunter Harmel. The FBIâs 521-day-long search finally came to a close thanks to a chance online meeting in Among Us, after the missing teen used the game to contact a fellow player for help.

As reported by Newsweek, the missing teenager was kidnapped by Harmel in May 2025. The predator supposedly lured the then 15-year-old victim into a meeting via Snapchat, at which point he disposed of her phone and kept her captive in his basement for over 500 days.

More recently, Harmel gave the victim a new phone without cellular data. However, the teenager was able to connect to Wi-Fi via the phone and used it to access the online game Among Us. The victim then seemingly used Among Usâ in-game chat function to contact another teenager in a lobby. The two exchanged social media details.

From here, the victim explained the details of her kidnapping and, according to Newsweekâs report, stated that âshe believed police had stopped looking for her.â The victimâs contact relayed this information to the teen’s mother, who ultimately contacted the FBI. Authorities then set up a video call with the kidnapped teenager, during which the victim recounted Harmelâs abuse during the 18 months she was held in captivity.

The FBI located the victim at Harmelâs residence in Oregon on November 1 and discovered multiple pieces of evidence that corroborated the kidnapped teenagerâs account, including a knife that Harmel reportedly used to threaten the victim.

Harmel was subsequently arrested during a traffic stop in Ontario, Oregon, after the 17-year-old victim was found in his home. Harmel was officially charged during a brief, 10-minute appearance at the U.S. District Courthouse in Boise, Idaho, on November 4.

The Airlines Offering Premium Economy On Airbus A380 Flights

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Brandon's passion for aviation started at a young age. He became involved in the flight simulator and VATSIM communities, which sparked his interest in aviation and eventually led him to earn his private pilot license. Outside of work, he continues to build flight hours, explore new airports, and stay involved in the aviation community.

Six of the 10 airlines still flying the Airbus A380 offer a premium economy cabin on the type. British Airways will shortly have the largest at 84 seats, following the retrofit announced on October 5, 2026, which also gives its aircraft the highest premium ratio of any A380 at roughly 48%. All Nippon Airways (ANA) follows at 73 seats, then Qantas at 60, Emirates at 56, Lufthansa at 52, and Singapore Airlines at 44.

The four without it are Qatar Airways, Etihad, Korean Air and Asiana. Qatar's layout of 8 first, 48 business and 461 economy produces a premium ratio of around 11%, the lowest among A380 operators. Where the cabin exists, the experience varies considerably. Emirates won the Skytrax award for best premium economy seat in 2026 and is adding the cabin to 68 A380 routes. ANA fits 73 premium economy seats against only 56 in business and flies them on a single route between Tokyo Narita International Airport (NRT) and Honolulu (HNL).

British Airways announced a full retrofit of its Airbus A380 fleet on October 5, 2026, and the new layout puts World Traveller Plus at 84 seats. That is the largest premium economy cabin on any A380 operating anywhere. The refurbished configuration runs 12 First, 106 Club Suites, 84 World Traveller Plus and 215 World Traveller for a total of 417 seats, down from 469 in the current layout. Economy absorbs the entire reduction, falling by 88 seats, while premium economy grows by more than half.

The proportions are what make the aircraft unusual. Head for Points calculated that the new layout gives British Airways 202 premium seats, roughly 48% of the aircraft, which is the highest premium ratio of any A380 operator by a wide margin. Emirates and Qantas are the next closest at around 30%, and both carry at least 56 fewer premium seats than the refurbished BA aircraft will. Since the A380 is the largest passenger aircraft ever built, that also gives British Airways the highest absolute number of premium seats on any single aircraft in service.

The timing matters for anyone trying to book. The first refurbished aircraft is expected in service within months, with the program running into 2027 and 2028, which means the 84-seat cabin will exist alongside the older 55-seat World Traveller Plus layout across the fleet for an extended period. British Airways operates 12 A380s to a small number of destinations from London Heathrow Airport (LHR), so the chance of drawing a refurbished aircraft on any given booking depends on how far along the program the fleet has progressed.

Emirates won the Skytrax award for the best premium economy seat in 2026, finishing ahead of Cathay Pacific and EVA Air, and The Points Guy named it Best International Premium Economy for a second consecutive year. AirlineRatings.com's passenger-voted Flyers' Choice Awards also named Emirates the preferred premium economy. The airline ranks second overall for premium economy with Skytrax and first for both the seats and the catering.

The A380 cabin seats 56 passengers within a four-class layout of 14 first, 76 business, 56 premium economy and 341 economy, giving the aircraft 146 premium seats and a premium ratio of around 30%. The seats are leather with adjustable headrests, calf rests, and footrests, and Emirates serves multicourse meals on china tableware with stainless steel cutlery, which is closer to a business class service pattern than most premium economy products attempt.

Scale is the other factor. Emirates operates roughly half the world's A380 fleet, with 116 aircraft, about 100 of which are active, and premium economy is arriving through the carrier's retrofit program rather than on new deliveries. The cabin joined Dubai to Milan on A380 services from November 10, 2025, alongside New York routes, and Emirates confirmed plans to offer premium economy on 68 routes by March 2026. Given the size of the fleet receiving it, a passenger searching for premium economy on a superjumbo will find more Emirates departures than from any other operator.

The cost of the world's longest A380 premium economy fares typically range within the $2,000-2,500, although this varies by discounts and season.

Qantas carries 60 premium economy seats on its A380s within a four-class layout of 14 first, 70 business, 60 premium economy and 341 economy, totaling 485 seats. That gives the aircraft 144 premium seats and a premium ratio of around 30%, effectively matching Emirates. The seats offer more width and legroom than economy, with adjustable headrests, footrests, storage and charging ports, and the fare includes priority check-in, priority boarding, upgraded meals, noise-canceling headphones and an amenity kit. Qantas operates 10 A380s, with around eight active, on routes from Sydney and Melbourne to London, Los Angeles, Dallas and Singapore.

Lufthansa fits 52 premium economy seats into a 509-seat A380 alongside 8 first, 78 business and 371 economy. The carrier operates eight aircraft and has committed to the type while retiring other quadjets, with summer 2026 routes including Los Angeles, Boston, Denver, Washington and Delhi, plus a new Munich to Mumbai service from July 2026. Lufthansa's premium economy ranks seventh overall in Skytrax's 2026 assessment and fifth for the seat specifically, though its highest-rated version is the Allegris cabin on the A350 and 787-9 rather than the A380.

Singapore Airlines has the smallest premium economy cabin on the type at 44 seats, in a 471-seat aircraft carrying 6 suites, 78 business and 343 economy. That is a premium ratio of around 27%, the lowest of the three. The product itself rates highly, placing third overall with Skytrax for premium economy and fourth for the seat, with calf and footrests, personal storage, charging ports and large entertainment screens. Premium economy passengers also get the Book the Cook service, allowing a main course to be reserved before departure, alongside priority check-in, boarding and baggage handling. The carrier operates 12 A380s across eight major routes from Changi for summer 2026.

All Nippon Airways carries 73 premium economy seats on its A380s, which ANA's own fleet material confirms in a 520-seat layout of 8 first, 56 business, 73 premium economy and 383 economy. That makes it the second-largest premium economy cabin on any A380, and Simple Flying reported it as the largest premium economy cabin across ANA's entire fleet. The seats sit at the rear of the upper deck behind first and business, arranged 2-3-2.

The aircraft itself is unlike any other A380 operation. ANA has three, each painted in a different sea turtle livery, and every scheduled A380 flight operates between Narita and Daniel K. Inouye International Airport (HNL) in Honolulu. No other operator confines the type to a single route. The aircraft entered service in May 2019 and continues to fly Narita to Honolulu year-round, with ANA adjusting frequencies seasonally. The third airframe only entered commercial service in late 2023 after an extended period in storage during the pandemic.

The configuration reflects who the aircraft carries. ANA built it around the Hawaii leisure market, and the cabin mix shows it, with 73 premium economy seats against 56 in business. The economy deck includes ANA COUCHii, where groups of three or four seats convert into a sofa-style space with raisable leg rests for an additional fee, which Airport Spotting described as aimed at families traveling with young children. For a passenger wanting premium economy on an A380, ANA offers one of the largest cabins available and the only route where you can find it.

A pro-Russian Serbian politician has allegedly caused a jet to return to Belgrade after dodging security

Four airlines fly the A380 with no premium economy cabin. Qatar Airways configures its aircraft with 8 first, 48 business and 461 economy seats across 517 total, giving it 56 premium seats and a premium ratio of around 11%, the lowest of any A380 operator by a substantial margin. Simple Flying excluded Qatar entirely from an earlier comparison of premium economy fares on the world's longest A380 routes, noting the airline does not offer the cabin on the type.

Etihad, Korean Air and Asiana complete the group. Etihad operates nine A380s and Asiana six, with Asiana running a three-class layout that includes 104 upper deck economy seats arranged 2-4-2. Those Asiana aircraft are also on borrowed time. Simple Flying reported that following the Korean Air merger, both carriers were expected to retire the type as part of fleet optimization.

The division is not a straightforward premium versus budget split. Qatar Airways has one of the most highly regarded business class products in the industry in Qsuite, yet its A380 carries the smallest proportion of premium seating of any operator of the type. A passenger wanting more than economy on a Qatar A380 has to buy business outright, with no intermediate option. Across the roughly 10 airlines still operating the A380, six offer premium economy and four do not, meaning the cabin is available on most superjumbo flights but not all of them.

25 Years Later, Buffy's Riskiest Episode Is Still a Brilliant Masterpiece That Changed the Show Forever

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

On Nov. 6, 2001, Buffy the Vampire Slayer made TV history. Again. The Emmy-winning series was already deep into its seven-season run when creator Joss Whedon came up with perhaps his most ambitious idea yet: a musical episode. The result was Buffy season 6 episode 7, "Once More, With Feeling," which remains a high point for the show and a frequent contender for best episode ever among diehard fans.

“Once More, With Feeling” isn't just a great musical (although it is) or a great episode of Buffy (it's also that). The brilliance of this 50-minute spectacle is how it combines the two, using a demon's curse that forces everyone in the town of Sunnydale to sing and dance as if they were in a Broadway play to push the show's overarching plot forward in numerous ways.

The best example of this may be the relationship between Buffy (Sarah Michelle Gellar) and Spike (James Marsters). The two had already been engaging in a toxic will-they-won't-they relationship for one season, and Spike takes the opportunity to get some big emotions off his chest while channeling Billy Idol in one of the musical’s best songs. At the end of the episode, when the curse is lifted but the revealed emotions still remain, Buffy and Spike share their first real kiss. Spoiler alert: their relationship does not go well.

But we're not here to relitigate "Spuffy." We're here to celebrate "Once More, With Feeling." To do so, I called up Marsters to recount the tale behind Buffy's iconic musical, from the terrifying moment he first found out about it to the episode's legacy 25 years later. Below, Polygon presents the behind-the-scenes story of "Once More, With Feeling" in the words of its breakout star.

You might think Joss Whedon would gather his cast to tell them they were about to make a musical. According to Marsters, he shared the news in a more abrupt way.

James Marsters: Instead of delivering the new script for the next episode, we were given a cassette tape with Joss singing the songs of the show and plunking them out on the piano. And whereas Joss is an enormously gifted writer and director and producer, as a singer he wasn't quite up to the material. As a piano player, he's fine, but it didn't sound like it was gonna work. It just sounded cheesy. I'm a punk rocker, and I was like, “What the F is this?”

I remember ripping the cassette tape out of the deck in my trailer and walking out into the bright sunshine and squinting and holding up this cassette tape, and everybody else was doing the same thing, opening their doors and just going, “What the fuck? Did you just listen to this? What's going on here?” It just set off this deep panic as we discovered that we were going to be asked to suddenly do a musical.

While a few of the cast members, including Marsters and Anthony Head (Giles) had professional singing experience, most did not.

Tony Head and I were both comfortable singing in public. I'd been singing in bars since I was 13. I was in a band, which I'm still in now, called Ghost of the Robot. Tony played Frank-N-Furter on the West End in Rocky Horror Picture Show. But the rest of the cast had a really good point, which is like, “I'm not a singer. You hired me to be a one-camera dramatic slash comedic actor, and that's what I do. And now you're gonna have me go out in front of tens of millions of people, maybe across the world, maybe a hundred million people, and do something that I'm not a professional at. You're gonna ruin my career.” That's actually not a bad point to make. I think Sarah asked to juggle chainsaws rather than have to sing — like that would be safer for her career.

So there's all this gnashing of teeth. And at some point, I think we all realized there was no way we were getting out of this. It was gonna happen no matter what we felt about it, which is a good thing. You don't wanna be asking actors all the time, “Are you comfortable doing this?” You don't want a TV show where the actors are in control. You want the producers and the writers to be in control if you want a good show.

Once the cast accepted their fate, they got to work and even brought in personal trainers to develop the skills they needed. However, it still took seeing an early cut of one sequence from the musical to win most of them over.

The reason why I'm so proud of us as a company, and why it is one of my favorite episodes, is that even though we were sure that we were going to go down in flames and all of our careers were over, even though we were sure that this show was going to get canceled very quickly after we tried this, we decided to go down swinging. We decided to try our best anyway, in the face of certain doom. People started hiring their own vocal coaches and their own dance instructors on their own dime. All these people were showing up to set. People were rehearsing and preparing in the middle of filming the previous episodes.

They knew we were freaking out. So the first thing they filmed was the Xander-Anya sequence, the dance and the song. They did a rough cut of that and showed it to us. And it was wonderful. It was amazing. We realized this thing actually was gonna be really good.

Marsters initially struggled to find a way to perform his big song, "Rest in Peace," but ultimately figured it out by taking some inspiration from an unlikely rock and roll icon. He also praises the song for expressing Spike's emotions at that point in the series in a way the character never would have been able to through normal written dialogue.

I didn't really know how to sing that song. So I started playing it on guitar, and I hit something in the way I was voicing the chords. It started working for me and I found my way in. I brought my guitar up to the production offices and I was like, “I found it! This is how we gotta do it,” and I started playing. The producers go, “That's amazing, James. That would be like if Nirvana did the song.” I was like, “Exactly!” And they said, “No, this is not Nirvana. This is like a Broadway rock song. So it's got to fit in. It is different from the other songs, but it's got to fit in that show.” And I was like, “F you guys. I'm gonna sing it that way anyway. Even if you're not gonna do the instrumentation, I'm gonna keep what I found.”

There's a lot of frustration pent up in Spike. Buffy has been kind of like, “Come here, get away from me. Come here, get away from me.” I have experienced that as a man, and I'm sure women experience the same thing. So I was like: Thank God I get to express it. How wonderful would it be to just break out in a song and say, "Babe, either take me or don't. You're just torturing me right now."

That's one of the many things that was genius about the idea. I heard long ago that for the best musicals, there comes a point in the dramatic story where the character wants to express something that words won't express. They need another tool to get there so they can tell the person what's really up. And song and dance are the only way to do it.

There's this lift in the story, in the character, in the emotion that happens when you break out into song. Joss knew that and he used that to get his characters to say things that they normally never would tell each other — secrets that people keep hidden from each other — and the chaos that can happen if that stuff is let out all in one day.

So why does this one episode hold up so well? For Marsters, it's not just because the songs are great and the plot is exciting, it's because it speaks to the inspiring theme at the heart of Buffy the Vampire Slayer.

The musical episode is most people's favorite episode of the show, and I think that's because it is frankly one of the best episodes of television that I'm aware of. It risked more. There was more work put into it. There was more creativity put into that episode than almost any other episode that I have ever seen. And I think the risk is really part of it.

The theme of Buffy is: Don't give up. It's hard to be a human being. It hurts to be here. The world is very imperfect, but don't give up on yourself. Don't give up on the world. Find something to fight for. What you decide to fight for is up to you. It might be taking care of your mom. It might be marching in the streets. But find the thing that's important to you and do it and don't give up on it. For one episode, we spoiled Hollywood actors lived up to the theme of the show. And then, luckily and wonderfully, it didn't suck.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

This flew so Doctor Horrible could soar and ask these years later it’s still the best thing anyone has ever self-published on the internet.

The renewable energy industry weighs in on the midterms

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Democratic legislators have wrested the majority of both the House and Senate from the Republicans’ grasp following a historic midterm election this week. The clean energy industry is clamoring for a sea change in federal support for renewables after the Trump administration razed incentives and funding since taking office in 2025.

While each candidate’s stance on clean energy isn’t apparent — nor is the effect this midterm election will have on domestic renewable energy markets — Democrats and left-leaning legislators have historically supported renewables more than their counterparts across the political aisle.

Wood Mackenzie is predicting that permitting reform will be a high priority for this new class of federal legislators, because of the rising costs of fuel and electricity from oil supply pressures brought on by the United States’ war with Iran and data center proliferation. However, the firm is also predicting that President Donald Trump could exercise his executive power to hamper proposed federal renewable energy subsidies.

“While we see the likelihood for permitting reform, how policy is legislated and implemented remains a risk to the U.S. economy,” said David Brown, director, global integrated research for Wood Mackenzie. “Entrenched partisanship could fuel policy stagnation and regulatory confusion.”

Representatives from the renewable energy industry and environmental organizations provided statements following the midterm results. Heres’ what they had to say.

Energy affordability was a key issue in this election, reflecting the real pressures facing American households and businesses. The solar and storage industry looks forward to working with newly elected federal and state leaders to bring more affordable, reliable power online and deliver the energy savings Americans are demanding.

– Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association

Families and businesses need affordable, reliable power, and strengthening America’s solar manufacturing base in a bipartisan manner will help deliver lower electricity bills. Over the past several years, American manufacturers have invested billions of dollars in new factories, expanded domestic production and created good-paying jobs in communities all across the country. With electricity demand rising, solar remains one of the fastest and most affordable ways to bring new power onto the grid, and the next Congress has an opportunity to ensure that power is American-made.

That starts with reinstating the domestic content incentives, so factories Congress helped bring online have a strong domestic market for American-made solar. It also means robust enforcement of our trade laws to ensure a level playing field for American solar manufacturers and continued investment in next-generation technologies so we can compete with, and leapfrog, China. We look forward to working with the new Congress to advance these shared priorities, keep electricity affordable for American families and strengthen our energy security and industrial workforce.

– Yogin Kothari, chief Strategy officer of the SEMA Coalition

Today, the clean power industry directly employs more than 400,000 Americans, and the U.S. has nearly 388 GW of clean power online, enough to power more than 83 million homes — but we’ll need a lot more than that to meet skyrocketing demand. Modernizing our outdated permitting process is key to unlocking over $800 billion in new infrastructure and 350-plus GW of new clean energy, keeping prices in check and the lights on at home for families and businesses across the country.

– Jason Grumet, CEO of the American Clean Power Association

Since their inception, competitive power markets have delivered billions in consumer savings while spurring innovation. They are the best way to make sure power generation investments are disciplined and efficient, with companies, not ratepayers, bearing the risk. EPSA looks forward to working with lawmakers on both sides of the aisle on policies that keep the grid reliable, protect customers and bring down costs in the long run.

– Todd Snitchler, president and CEO of the Electric Power Supply Association

The message from voters is clear: Americans want greater economic security, relief from rising costs and an energy system that works for them. This election put a bright spotlight on how this country’s outdated energy system and dependence on fossil fuels leave families and businesses exposed to unaffordable price shocks … Some of the biggest opportunities for progress may come at the state and city level. New governors, state legislatures and mayors have the power to move quickly on solutions that address the needs of their constituents: strengthening local economies, creating jobs and lowering bills. From ensuring data centers provide real benefits to communities to expanding clean energy, modernizing the grid, enabling affordable and accessible housing, and improving public transit, state and local leaders can walk-the-talk that climate action delivers real benefits for people.

– David Widawsky, director of World Resources Institute United States

Regardless of election outcomes, access to affordable, reliable energy and energy infrastructure remains essential to nearly every aspect of our daily lives, from powering our homes and businesses to keeping the cost of everyday goods and services within reach. As these leaders prepare to take office, we encourage them to make energy access, affordability, and reliability a priority.

We are ready to work with policymakers on both sides of the aisle to advance policies that support the development of critical energy infrastructure to create jobs, strengthen our nation’s energy security and ensure families and businesses have access to the energy they need.

– David Holt, president of the Consumer Energy Alliance

We just witnessed the collective power voters showed to beat back the Trump regime and fight for their country. Trump and his GOP cronies tried to drive our economy into the ground, but the American people stood up and made it clear that they reject Trump’s disastrous agenda.

We are committed to working with the incoming Congress to hold the administration accountable for the harm they have done to working people and pass legislation to build a clean economy that works for us all — not just billionaires. Creating opportunity for workers and communities while addressing environmental challenges is not just good policy, it’s also good politics.

– Jason Walsh, executive director of the BlueGreen Alliance

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.
