the EU bans the destruction of unsold fashion, but is it enough to stop luxury waste?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

new european regulations challenge fashion’s take-make-waste model, but important exclusions leave questions about how far the industry is willing to change.

The European Union is banning fashion companies from destroying unsold clothing, accessories, and footwear, introducing new restrictions on large businesses under its Ecodesign for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR). The rules, which came into application earlier this year, require companies to explore alternatives such as resale, donation, repair, and reuse instead of destroying surplus stock.

The measure targets a long-standing practice in the fashion industry, where perfectly usable products have sometimes been discarded to manage excess inventory and preserve brand exclusivity. While the ban marks an important step towards reducing textile waste, exclusions for certain luxury goods leave us wondering:  how much can legislation change an industry whose business model continues to depend on producing more?

According to the European Environment Agency (EEA), an estimated 4 to 9 percent of all textile products placed on the European market are destroyed before they are even used. This amounts to between 264,000 and 594,000 tons of textiles every year. The figures include returned and unsold products, revealing the scale of resources invested in objects that never fulfill their intended purpose. Every garment carries the footprint of its production, from raw material extraction and processing to dyeing, manufacturing, packaging, and transportation. When a perfectly functional item is destroyed, the resources invested in making it are effectively wasted.

The problem is not limited to fast fashion. Luxury brands have also come under scrutiny for destroying unsold products, sometimes to protect their exclusivity and control where their goods are sold. This is particularly contradictory for an industry that places such value on craftsmanship, quality, and long-lasting products. According to the EEA, overproduction is a major problem in the textile industry. Brands often produce more than they expect to sell to ensure they have enough stock to meet demand. But when these products remain unsold, the environmental cost of making them does not simply disappear.

The ban forms part of the Ecodesign for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR), which came into force in 2024 as part of the EU’s broader strategy to make products more durable, repairable, recyclable, and resource-efficient. Since July 19, 2026, large companies operating in the EU have been prohibited from destroying unsold apparel, clothing accessories, and footwear covered by the regulation. Medium-sized companies will become subject to the same prohibition from July 2030, while micro and small enterprises are exempt.

Businesses must come up with alternatives to destroying surplus stock that keep products in circulation. These include selling unsold items through discounted or alternative markets, donating them to charities and social enterprises, and preparing them for reuse through repair, refurbishment, or remanufacturing. The legislation also introduces greater accountability. Companies must disclose information about discarded unsold products, provide supporting documentation when relying on permitted exceptions, and retain records for five years so national authorities can monitor compliance. Destruction is not entirely prohibited, but it must fall within specific derogations and follow the applicable waste treatment hierarchy, which gives priority to recycling. The intention is to move away from a linear model in which products are manufactured, sold, and discarded, towards a system that recognizes their continued material and functional value.

Despite its ambitions, the regulation does not cover every product category, and this is where its limitations become particularly relevant to the luxury industry. One notable exclusion concerns leather handbags and other similar containers. Although leather clothing and clothing accessories are included in the ban, handbags fall under a separate category, which is not included in the list of products whose destruction is prohibited. An unsold leather jacket falls within the prohibition, but an unsold leather handbag does not. This is not a special exemption for luxury brands, but a consequence of how the legislation defines the product categories covered by the ban. Nevertheless, it leaves a substantial category of high-value fashion goods outside this particular restriction.

The regulation also allows destruction under specific circumstances. These include products that are dangerous, do not comply with applicable legislation, or are found to infringe intellectual property rights. Products that have suffered damage, deterioration, or contamination that makes them unsuitable for consumer use may also qualify when repair or refurbishment is not technically feasible or cost-effective. Another particularly interesting provision concerns products that cannot be prepared for reuse or remanufacturing because it is technically impossible to remove or permanently conceal protected or otherwise inappropriate labels, logos, recognizable designs, or other characteristics.

For brands whose commercial value depends heavily on recognizable logos, signatures, and protected designs, this provision deserves attention. However, it is not a blanket exemption allowing companies to destroy unwanted merchandise simply to protect their image. The rules also permit destruction when products have been offered for donation to at least three suitable social economy organizations within the EU or through an accessible company website for at least eight weeks without being accepted. Even products already donated can eventually be destroyed if no recipient can be found.

Perhaps the most important limitation is that the new legislation addresses what happens to unsold products rather than why so many are produced in the first place. A brand can comply with the ban while continuing to manufacture more garments than the market can absorb. It can discount, redistribute, donate, or redirect excess inventory towards alternative markets without fundamentally changing its production volumes.

There is also a question about the effectiveness of reuse and donation as long-term solutions. Redirecting products does not automatically guarantee that they will be worn, repaired, or incorporated in a meaningful way into another market. Without careful management, surplus goods can simply move through additional distribution channels before eventually becoming waste elsewhere. The EEA has documented how surplus and used textiles exported from Europe enter complex international trade networks. While many are intended for reuse or recycling, some eventually end up in landfills, are burned, or are dumped in countries with inadequate waste management infrastructure.

Moving excess stock out of sight does not necessarily resolve its environmental impact. A circular approach would require more than alternative destinations for unwanted products. It would involve reconsidering production quantities, improving demand forecasting, designing garments for longer use, investing in repair infrastructure, and making the entire lifecycle of a product more transparent.

The EU’s ban is a step in the right direction, but it also raises a question about the way luxury fashion works. Some brands choose to destroy unsold clothes rather than sell them at a much lower price. The concern is that lower prices could damage the image of exclusivity they have built around their products. In other words, clothes that could still be worn are destroyed because selling them for less is seen as worse for the brand.

Still, the problem is not just what happens to unsold clothes but how many are made in the first place. The new rules may stop companies from destroying some of their surplus stock, but they do not address the pressure to keep producing more. Until that changes, the industry will keep finding itself with more clothes than it can sell.

Tesla will stop making the Cybertruck in 2027

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Tesla will stop making the Cybertruck next year, according to CEO Elon Musk, who said on X that the company will use the factory space to build more two-seater Cybercab robotaxis.

The decision to wind down the futuristic, steel-clad EV comes after just a few short years of underwhelming sales. When Musk handed over the first few Cybertrucks in late 2023, he predicted Tesla would sell around 250,000 per year. It was supposed to be such a cornerstone of Tesla’s future that, before construction began, the company talked about its Texas factory as the “Cybertruck factory.” But Tesla has instead sold just tens of thousands — total — over the last three years.

Tesla is killing off the Cybertruck at a moment when it is trying to become an AI- and robotics-focused company. A big part of that is its robotaxi ambitions.

The company has spent the last two months putting Cybercabs on the streets in its hometown of Austin, Texas. With no major incidents to date, the company has said it plans to expand the Cybercabs operations to other cities, starting with Dallas later this month. (Tesla also operates robotaxi versions of its Model Y SUVs in Austin, Houston, and Dallas, as well as three cities in Florida.)

This is the third product Tesla has discontinued in order to fuel those greater ambitions. Earlier this year, the company decided to end production of the Model S sedan and Model X SUV and instead use the factory space dedicated to those vehicles to make Optimus humanoid robots.

First announced in 2019, the Cybertruck was a product that Musk had long obsessed over.

“Probably my personal favorite for the next product is the pickup truck, and we are going to just do an amazing pickup truck,” he said on one of Tesla’s earnings calls that year.

Musk promised the Cybertruck would be radical, inspired by the designs of cyberpunk sci-fi stories like Blade Runner. While that certainly bore out in the design, the company overpromised on the basics of the Cybertruck from the jump. During the now-infamous reveal event in 2019, when head designer Franz von Holzhausen cracked the Cybertruck’s windows with steel balls, Tesla said the EV was supposed to start at under $40,000 and have up to 500 miles of range. It achieved neither of those figures.

Instead, the truck was delayed for two years, in part because of the Covid-19 pandemic. When it finally did hit the market in late 2023, Tesla handed over the first few Cybertrucks to customers in a short, somewhat odd for Tesla event (by the company’s own standards, at least), which saw a poorly-lit Musk standing in a truck bed promising the truck was “the finest in apocalyptic equipment” fit for “late civilization vibes.” The base price had inflated to around $60,000, and the top-end range had shrunk by over 100 miles.

The truck has struggled on the market ever since. The polarizing design never seemed to sit well with buyers, especially at a higher-than-expected price. Then, during its first full year of sales in 2024, Musk became a major financial backer of Donald Trump, which turned the instantly recognizable Cybertruck into a target for protesters. That continued into 2025 as Musk led the so-called Department of Government Efficiency, which fired thousands of federal workers.

'Big Bang' Fix Puts Voyager Across the 'One Light-Day' Mark

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

An innovative solution helps to put the venerable Voyager 1 spacecraft past a key milestone.

Flashback to 1977. Jimmy Carter was U.S. president, Star Wars was a box office hit, and the two Voyager spacecraft departed on an historic Grand Tour of the solar system. Now, teams at NASAâs Jet Propulsion Laboratory are working to keep the missions active, as they push the boundaries of interstellar space beyond the solar system.

NASA recently announced that after a brief bit of drama and loss of communications with Voyager 1, a fix dubbed the âBig Bangâ was successful in resuscitating the spacecraft. The Big Bang swap alternated three units (two primary heaters and an old 8-track digital tape recorder) with three low power secondary options. The brief loss of communications occurred on October 20th, and lasted for 48 hours.

Engineers work on a Voyager spacecraft prior to launch. Credit: NASA/JPL-Caltech

âThis is the first time weâve seen the spacecraft do something like this, but we are also at a point in the mission where we have to expect the unexpected,â said Kareem Badaruddin, in a recent press release. âWeâre relieved to be back in contact with Voyager 1 and are eager to understand what happened.â

The Big Bang method was also successfully implemented to keep Voyager 2 functioning this past August.

Traveling far from the Sun, the Voyager missions rely on Plutonium-fueled radioisotope thermoelectric generators for power. PU-238 has a half-life of just under 88 years, meaning the missions are now steadily losing power.

This loss equates to about 4 watts of power each year. Voyager 1 currently utilizes a little over 200 watts of power, about enough to power an electric blanket. This has forced mission controllers to make some tough choices, including turning off the Low Energy Charged Particle experiment LECP in early 2026. The missions launched with 10 instruments in 1977.

The swaps had to occur at the same time, as the units involved also heat the spacecraft and assure that it continues to operate in the chilly depths of deep space.

Voyager 1 launched on September 5, 1977, actually after Voyager 2. Voyager 1 flew past Jupiter in 1979 and Saturn in 1980. A flyby past enigmatic Titan to get a close look on November 12, 1980 meant the mission would depart the plane of the solar system afterwards. Voyager 2 went on to pass Uranus in 1986 and Neptune in 1989.

The paths of Voyagers 1 and 2 out of the solar system. Credit: NASA/JPL-Caltech

The Voyagers continue to do science as they travel through interstellar space beyond the fringes of the outer solar system. The two Voyagers are the first missions to sample the region of space beyond the heliosphere directly.

The Voyagers head out beyond the bowshock of the solar system into interstellar space. Credit: NASA

Right now, the important Plasma Wave Subsystem (PWS) plus a magnetometer instrument on Voyager 1 remain active. Without the Big Bang shuffle, mission controllers would have had to make the hard decision turn off yet another crucial instrument in 2027.

Voyager 1 witnesses a 'crescent Saturn'. Credit: NASA/JPL-Caltech

Moving at 16.9 kilometers per second relative to the Sun and the solar system, Voyager 1 will become the first human-made artifact to reach the one light-day mark this month on Wednesday, November 18th at 5:16 AM EST. Thatâs roughly 26 billion kilometers or 172 Astronomical Units (AU) distant. For context, the Moon and cis-lunar space marking the farthest humans have traveled to date is just over one light-second distant.

NASAâs global Deep Space Network continues to monitor the ever fainter signals from the Voyager spacecraft. Controllers will likely have to contend with more signal outages in the years to come.

The worldwide DSN tracks missions including one of the Voyager spacecraft. Credit: NASA/DSN

Along with the now silent Pioneers 10 and 11 and the still active New Horizons mission, Voyagers 1 and 2 are now leaving the solar system for galactic space. The upper stage Star boosters that help these missions achieve escape velocity are on their way outbound as well. The next mission escaping the solar system wonât happen for a while, though the proposed Interstellar Probe mission could one day make the trip. Voyager 1 is now outbound in the direction of the constellation Ophiuchus near its border with Hercules.

Congrats the intrepid and resourceful Voyager 1 team. Hopefully the mission will surpass its half-a-century birthday in space, coming right up next year.

David Dickinson is a freelance science writer and long-time sky watcher. He has built telescopes and observatories, chased eclipses, and travels and observes with his wife, Myscha, on a mission to get âeyes on the skyâ worldwide. His books The Universe Today Ultimate Guide to Observing the Cosmos, The Astronomerâs Deep-Sky Field Guide and science fiction short stories are available here.

Zoomlion says its new, 1,600 hp hybrid tractor is the world’s most powerful

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Chinese equipment brand Zoomlion showed up to this week’s China International Agricultural Machinery Exhibition with a new ultra-high-horsepower hybrid tractor that sets a new benchmark, offering more than 1,600 hp while cutting back on both fuel and fumes.

Zoomlion calls its new 9DX hybrid tractor a radical rethink of what a modern ag unit can be, combining the all-day pulling power of a conventional diesel machine with the efficiency, accuracy, and instant torque benefits of electric drive. The company claims its hybrid setup can reducing fuel consumption, emissions, and deliver lower operating costs, without asking massive industrial farming operations to walk away from diesel entirely.

The tractor itself gets a headline-grabbing 1,600 hp Zoomlion 9DX features an 800V hybrid electric powertrain and continuously variable transmission that delivering a peak torque of 7,200 Nm (~5,300 lb-ft) of torque. Getting all that power to the ground is a four track tank-drive system riding on Zoomlion’s in-house developed 762 mm (~30 inch) wide tracks that are designed to distribute the tractor’s considerable weight for maximum traction and minimum soil compaction. What’s more, the high-traction treads can push the big machine to hit a maximum speed of 40 km/h across grades and slopes of up to 12 degrees.

The 9DX also gets a high-capacity hydraulic system capable of delivering 636 liters per minute of flow (~170 GPM), allowing it to work with some seriously heavy duty implements like ultra-wide seeders and variable-rate fertilizer applicators.

The Zoomlion 9DX was one of several new machines at this year’s exhibition, which hosted more than 3,000 exhibitors and vendors – but the big hp numbers all over the show made an even bigger impression, leading AGCO’s director of global electronic and electrical engineering, Nicolas Meunier, to tell Chinese State news agency Xinhua that the country had moved on from producing mostly low-horsepower tractors to building some seriously large machines.

There’s no official word on specs and batteries, but the company has developed an 800V modular battery pack system for its other equipment offerings that can scale from 140-800 kWh of capacity while other top-shelf Zoomlion tractors featuring an ag version of Cummins’ 15 liter turbodiesel.

The 9DX tops off what’s sure to be another record year for Zoomlion, which is coming off strong early export quarters shipping into Germany, Australia, and the United Arab Emirates (UAE).

Malick Yalcouye: The unlikely Brighton star nobody saw coming

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Brighton spent more than £200m in the summer of 2024. Big names and established internationals arrived on the south coast but hidden among them was a £6m arrival from IFK Goteborg called Malick Yalcouye.

The Mali-born midfielder had made just 14 appearances for IFK but Tony Bloom's algorithms and data models must have been pinging notifications left, right centre - which is quite apt, as Yalcouye can play left, right and centre.

Did you know with an ad-lite subscription to SussexWorld, you get 70% fewer ads while viewing the news that matters to you.

The slightly-built 5ft 7in midfielder signed a five-year contract with Brighton and was immediately sent on loan to Sturm Graz.

He made 36 appearances for the Austrians -including eight in the Champions League - and scored four goals with two assists.

It was a solid seasons work and the next step was the second tier of English football and a loan move to Swansea.

Alan Sheehan was the head coach at the start of the season for the Swans but he was replaced by Vitor Matos, who was the elite development coach at Liverpool under Jürgen Klopp.

Yalcouye totalled 36 outings for Swansea and helped them to an 11th placed finish but it's fair to say the Brighton man had a mixed loan spell in south Wales.

His performances did little to suggest he would be such a force for Brighton in the Premier League or Conference League this season.

But the Seagulls have been here before. Yasin Ayari under-performed while on loan at Blackburn and Coventry - in fact the Sweden international described his stint at Coventry as "total shit" but he is now a machine in the Premier League for Brighton and a potential £60m-plus player.

Yalcouye, 20, probably isn't as harsh as Ayari in his assessment from his season in the Championship but his start to the current campaign has taken his career to a new level.

He has featured in every match in all competitions so far. He has played every minute of the Conference League phase and started both Carabao Cup matches away at Manchester United and Manchester City.

He has started three in the Premier League and scored in two of those - both of which were excellent goals at Chelsea and Coventry.

He also scored in the Carabao at City and netted a powerful header in Brighton's 1-0 Conference League win at Getafe last night.

Albion boss Fabian Hurzeler, speaking after Thursday night's win at the Estadio Coliseum, said: “Malick has different skills and one skill is that he can attack the box well.

"He didn't only show it today or prove it today. He proved it through the whole season."

Yalcouye's stature and running power has drawn comparisons with former Leicester, Chelsea and France midfielder N'golo Kante but Yalcouye is different.

He can play the deeper midfield role but using him in advanced positions is a very clever move from Hurzeler and that's what has taken many by surprise.

Brighton's attacking players have to defend and press relentlessly high up the pitch and few players do that as well Yalcouye.

At first you think he's in the team for his running power alone. Then you realise, actually he's technically pretty good as well. He's also calm in front of goal and for a small player he can header with the best of them - his excellent goal at Getafe was case in point.

But surely his slight physic is going to be a problem when it comes to one-one duels in Premier League? Nope.

"Everyone looks at him and thinks he's too small," said Brighton skipper Lewis Dunk to Sussex World.

Dunk also stated that Yalcouye is "shy" lad but the squad are helping him grow in confidence and develop, which is what Brighton do best.

Yalcouye is one of the best stories to emerge from Brighton this season. He's a unique talent and has made it to the top level against the odds... and with a little help from Bloom’s data model.

His contract runs until 2029 and no doubt Brighton are already looking at that and considering improved terms.

Yalcouye's £6m arrival in 2024 went beneath the radar, but not anymore. He has a huge role to play as Brighton look to achieve something special this season.

Moderna raises 2026 outlook as CEO says newer vaccines are gaining traction

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Moderna on Friday smashed third-quarter revenue and earnings estimates and hiked its outlook for full-year sales growth, as some of its newer products gain momentum and Covid vaccine revenue outperforms.

Shares of Moderna rose roughly 3% in premarket trading on Friday.

The biotech company now expects 2026 revenue growth of 15% to 25%, up from its previous guidance of up to 10%.

"The new product and the diversification of geography is what has led to such a strong Q3," Moderna CEO Stéphane Bancel said in an interview ahead of the results.

For example, he said the company is seeing the "full-year impact" of its next-generation mRNA Covid vaccine, which won U.S. approval in May 2025. Bancel noted that Covid is a seasonal business and vaccine contracts are typically established in the first quarter, so Moderna's shot only had a "small launch" to start with last year.

Moderna's new stand-alone flu shot, which was approved in the U.S. in August, will similarly have a "limited launch" in the country in 2026 given the timing, he said.

In a separate interview on CNBC's "Squawk Box," Bancel said Moderna's geographic expansion in Canada, the U.K. and Asia helped boost results. The company's work to lower costs also resulted in a profit in the quarter, he added.

"It's the sales that are stronger and the costs that are also lower," Bancel said.

Here's what Moderna reported for the third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Moderna's third-quarter revenue jumped 29% to $1.31 billion, boosted by higher Covid vaccine sales in international markets and helped by deliveries under long-term government partnerships. Revenue was $780 million in the U.S. and $533 million in international markets.

The company posted a net income of $162 million, or 38 cents per share, for the third quarter. That compares with a net loss of $200 million, or a loss of 51 cents per share, reported for the year-ago period.

The results come weeks after Moderna and Merck unveiled promising late-stage data on their experimental personalized cancer vaccine in combination with immunotherapy Keytruda in certain melanoma patients. Despite the positive results, investors battered Moderna's stock after part of the data was leaked on social media just days before trial investigators were set to present it at a European medical conference.

Bancel called it a "surprising reaction," but said that the companies also saw a positive response from others on Wall Street, including long-term investors and analysts who examined the full data in depth. He added that the doctors in the field "were so impressed with the data," and many expressed that they hope to use it in their day-to-day practice.

Moderna's stock has skyrocketed more than 700% this year, largely on optimism about the potential for the shot.

Nick Walker Built Olympia-winning Quads with this Leg Press Tweak

Nick Walker brought his most impressive physique yet to the stage during the 2026 Mr. Olympia final, earning his first Sandow trophy and proving that hard work really does pay off. And, in a recent Instagram post, the man they call “The Mutant” shared one of the reasons for his success, explaining that he utilizes an angled stance on the leg press to build his outer sweep. Here’s why.

“This is my bread and butter,” said Walker, explaining the importance of the leg press in his overall lower body strategy. “This is what I believe has put the majority of my new leg size on, and I know I get a lot of criticism for the way I do it. Feet wide, angled out. That works better for me, because when I go wide, toes angled out more than usual, I know we get a lot more knee flexion, and I’m able to contract the outer sweep of my legs.”

In the video, posted for his two-million-plus Instagram followers, “The Mutant” can be seen sans shoes in order to make a better connection with the platform. His toes are pointed outwards, like the hands on a clock showing ten to two. This serves to flare his legs out, but his legs are actually no wider apart than his hips.

Walker explained that hitting the outer sweep of his legs was an important part of his Olympia puzzle, and he feels that this technique served him well. While experts often suggest that a narrow stance works better for targeting the outer thigh muscles, “The Mutant” says that this just does not work for him. So, if you are finding it difficult to add mass to your sweep with a narrow stance, the champ’s variation might work for you.

Walker makes sure to go as deep as he can during the negative portion of the press. “I like to pause right in the stretch for a hot second,” said The Mutant. “Explode up, low negatives … I believe that has helped me tremendously.”

But what about those naysayers who comment that the Mr. Olympia winner is pressing the wrong way? “Yes, there will be some hamstring involvement,” said Walker. “Yes, there will be some adductor involvement, but that’s okay. Because I am getting what I need out of it, which is to target the outer sweep. If another couple of muscles are involved… not the end of the world.”

DSA’s Adrian Wall and Former CFTC Chair Chris Giancarlo Discuss Future of U.S. Crypto Regulation at Strobe Summit

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Washington, D.C., November 6, 2026 — The Digital Sovereignty Alliance (DSA), a nonprofit organization dedicated to advancing clear and ethical public policy, research, and education related to emerging technologies, participated in the Strobe Summit, where DSA Managing Director Adrian Wall moderated a fireside chat with former Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) Chair Christiopher  Giancarlo on the evolving regulatory landscape for digital assets and the future of financial markets.

Titled “Crypto at a Crossroads: Regulation, Institutional Adoption, and the Future of Financial Markets,” the discussion examined the path forward for U.S. digital asset policy amid legislative uncertainty and growing institutional interest in blockchain-based financial infrastructure. Drawing on his experience leading the CFTC and founding the Digital Dollar Project, Giancarlo emphasized an approach to regulation that combines clear exchange rules under existing CFTC authority with congressional action to address remaining gaps. The conversation also explored the implications of the GENIUS Act, including Giancarlo’s concerns about financial privacy and his view that dollar-backed stablecoins could become a 24/7 global payment rail, raising fundamental questions about how the dollar moves on-chain and who provides that infrastructure.

Wall and Giancarlo also examined the regulatory challenges surrounding prediction markets, perpetual futures, and decentralized finance (DeFi). Topics included the CFTC’s role in overseeing event contracts, bringing offshore derivatives activity into regulated U.S. markets, the potential for 24/7 trading, and establishing accountability for decentralized platforms without traditional intermediaries. The discussion highlighted the need for regulatory frameworks that address market integrity, cybersecurity, customer asset protection, and the operational realities of increasingly interconnected financial markets.

“Regulatory clarity requires more than new legislation. It requires rules that reflect how digital asset markets actually operate,” said Adrian Wall, moderator of the discussion. “The CFTC has existing authority to address important market structure questions, while Congress has a critical role in resolving regulatory gaps. Getting that balance right will determine whether the U.S. can bring more digital asset activity onshore, accelerate institutional adoption, and protect market participants without compromising innovation or financial privacy.”

DSA’s participation reflects its commitment to advancing informed policy dialogue and practical regulatory approaches that support responsible innovation, market integrity, and the integration of digital assets into the broader financial system.

The Digital Sovereignty Alliance (DSA) is a nonprofit social welfare organization committed to advocating for public policies that support ethical innovation in decentralized technologies, blockchain, cryptocurrency, Web3, and artificial intelligence. DSA conducts research, organizes educational events, and promotes policies that prioritize public welfare and digital sovereignty.

Nintendo has a long history of taking down projects it deems to infringe on its rights, while emulators such as the now defunct suyu often argue that they do not circumvent Nintendo's privacy measures.

Bank of England pours cold water on AI productivity boom

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Turns out throwing billions at GPUs doesn't automatically mean everyone gets more done

Despite the billions being thrown at artificial intelligence, the technology has delivered little in the way of productivity gains for UK businesses, according to the Bank of England.

In its latest Monetary Policy Report, the central bank found that AI has increased UK productivity by an estimated 0.5 percent over the past three years. That works out to just 0.1 to 0.2 percentage points of additional annual productivity growth since early 2024, hardly the economic revolution Silicon Valley has been promising.

"AI investment appears to be contributing somewhat to current demand strength, but so far there is only evidence of a modest AI-driven rise in UK productivity growth," the Bank said.

Even industries supposedly best placed to benefit from the technology have little to boast about. Productivity growth has improved in AI-heavy sectors such as information and communications, but has yet to exceed pre-pandemic averages.

That's not to say AI isn't doing anything for the British economy. The bank estimates that AI-related activity has accounted for just under a fifth of UK GDP growth since the beginning of 2026, though much of that appears to come from companies developing and deploying the technology rather than businesses becoming more productive by using it.

"The contribution to growth from AI to date appears to reflect activity in the upstream development and implementation of AI technologies rather than broader gains from their adoption elsewhere in the economy," the report said. In other words, selling AI is proving rather more lucrative than actually using it,

The bank also cautioned against giving AI too much credit for Britain's recent productivity improvements. Much of the increase appears to reflect businesses hiring fewer workers while output continues to grow, partly as companies unwind post-pandemic staffing decisions and respond to higher employment costs.

And despite predictions that AI will make vast swathes of the workforce redundant, firms responding to the Bank's Decision Maker Panel survey reported virtually no overall impact on employment so far. The technology is, however, making some employers less inclined to hire new staff or replace those who leave.

Other studies suggest businesses are still waiting for the payoff. A Gartner survey published last month found that while three-quarters of corporate board members expected AI to improve productivity and cut costs, just 3 percent of organizations had actually achieved their targets. Meanwhile, 38 percent were already over budget on AI spending, yet 85 percent planned to spend even more next year.

Another study involving nearly 6,000 executives, published in February, found that more than 80 percent of firms had seen no discernible impact from AI on either employment or productivity. Apparently, the answer to disappointing returns is to keep writing checks.

That growing disconnect between AI spending and actual returns has also caught the attention of financial watchdogs. The International Monetary Fund warned last month that the AI spending spree could come back to bite investors if the promised profits don't show up, potentially dragging the wider economy down with it.

The Bank for International Settlements sounded a similar alarm in June, comparing the current spending spree to the dotcom boom of the 1990s. Its concern is that tech giants are committing enormous sums to infrastructure on the assumption that AI will eventually deliver equally enormous returns, leaving them exposed if reality fails to cooperate.

However, AI is already making its presence felt in one area: prices. The Bank of England noted that surging demand for AI-related products, including memory chips, has pushed up export prices in China and other Asian economies, raising import costs for Britain.

So while businesses are still waiting for their AI-powered productivity windfall, the costs of the boom are already making their way through the economy.

The bank isn't writing off the technology just yet. Businesses surveyed expect AI to add around 0.9 percentage points to annual productivity growth over the next three years, with the gains split roughly equally between faster output growth and slower employment growth.

It seems the AI industry's most impressive productivity achievement is convincing businesses to keep opening their wallets. ®

BSC Releases Reth Exploit Hotfix: Details About the Upgrade

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

BNB Smart Chain (BSC) has released a hotfix to its Reth execution client, BSC-Reth, according to the official announcement and a patch on Github. The patch addresses a vulnerability discovered on November 4, 2026, which could result in incorrect blockchain state-root calculations due to a bug in the legacy trie code (used to organize blockchain data).

Although the vulnerability could have resulted in financial loss, there is no evidence that the vulnerability was exploited or that funds were affected.

Rust Ethereum Harvester Tree, also known as Reth, is an execution client used to verify the data on the blockchain and process transactions. On November 4, 2026, a vulnerability was discovered in certain versions of Reth that could result in incorrect state-root calculations due to a bug in the legacy trie code.

This could potentially result in node disagreements and sync failures. The underlying bug was fixed in Reth pull request #28264, merged on November 3.

On November 6, BNB Chain published BSC-Reth v0.1.4-hotfix on GitHub. According to the release notes, this version includes a fix for incorrect state-root calculation in the legacy path in Reth. This version of BSC-Reth improves the way it deals with blockchain data, reducing the risk of incorrect state-root calculation during state verification.

All BSC-Reth node operators, including validators, MEV builders, full nodes, and sentry nodes, are advised to upgrade to this version and restart the node as soon as possible. This fix does not require a DB migration or hard fork. Users do not need to take any action. BNB Chain has also confirmed that this vulnerability has a limited effect on BSC-Reth and has no effect on opBNB.

Nigeria’s central bank introduces stablecoin licensing rules, bans algorithmic versions

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The rules took effect on Nov. 5 and cover virtual asset activities conducted in Nigeria, directed at Nigerian users, or affecting the country's financial system.

The Central Bank of Nigeria has introduced new licensing requirements for stablecoin issuers and other non-security virtual asset service providers, according to regulations that took effect on Thursday.

According to a circular issued to relevant stakeholders, the regulations establish separate licensing categories for naira-referenced and foreign-currency-referenced stablecoins. Issuers must also secure approval for each token before beginning issuance.

The central bank said the rules cover activities carried out in Nigeria or affecting the country's payments system, financial sector, or foreign exchange market, even if the operator has no physical presence in the country.

Under the framework, foreign-currency stablecoins can only be offered, distributed, exchanged, or otherwise made available to Nigerians as Class 2 tokens issued through a Nigerian-incorporated entity holding a Foreign Currency Issuer Licence.

The CBN also requires issuers to obtain written approval for each token, covering its classification, reference currency, reserve arrangements, name, ticker, and supported blockchain networks, per the circular.

The rules also enforce compliance with anti-money laundering and counter-terrorist financing obligations. Entities must implement customer due diligence, sanctions screening, transaction monitoring, and Travel Rule compliance across all custodial wallets, peer-to-peer services, and remittance channels.

Meanwhile, some tokens are prohibited under the rules, including algorithmic stablecoins. The central bank said the ban covers tokens that rely primarily on market dynamics, rather than eligible reserve assets, to maintain their value.

The framework gives the CBN authority over stablecoins and virtual assets used for payments, transfers, remittances, foreign exchange, and settlement. The Securities and Exchange Commission retains oversight of securities and capital-market activities.

Companies operating across both areas must obtain the relevant approvals from each regulator. The regulations took effect on Nov. 5, 2026, with existing operators given 180 days to apply for the appropriate licence or authorization, subject to transitional requirements.

Bitcoin Price Falls Ahead of Jobs Report: Is a Fourth Sell-Off Inbound?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin news: BTC closed below its 200-day EMA before the jobs report. It rose after 52 of 104 reports, but fell after the last 3.

In Bitcoin news on Friday, November 6, BTC heads into the US October jobs report at 8:30 a.m. ET after closing Thursday at $76,088. BTC USD price is now below its 200-day average for the first time since August 19.

The report itself has been a coin toss. The recent record has not.

🚨 MARKET ALERT: U.S. UNEMPLOYMENT DATA DROPS TODAY AT 8:30 AM ET! 🇺🇸

One number could influence today's market reaction.

📊 Unemployment Rate• Previous: 4.2%• Forecast: 4.2%Here's what I'm watching:🔹 Above 4.2% → A weaker labor market could ease rate-hike concerns and… pic.twitter.com/6Zu5ewzubQ

— OwenCarter (@OwenCarter0el) November 6, 2026

A Coinspeaker review of all 104 jobs reports since January 2018 shows Bitcoin was higher 24 hours later after exactly 52 of them. A week later, though, it was lower after each of the last three: down 3.3% in August, 5.2% in September and 3.7% in October.

The setup is already weak. Bitcoin trades at $76,160, down 5.1% in 24 hours, after Thursday’s chip-stock sell-off dragged it from $80,918 to $75,208, its lowest since September 16. The Crypto Fear & Greed Index fell from 50 to 28 in a day.

The jobs data also feeds straight into the Fed. Traders on Polymarket now price a 69% chance of a rate hike on December 9, down from 80% a day earlier.

So what does Bitcoin usually do after a jobs report, and can it win back the 200-day line?

Market Intelligence: Crypto Analyst Predicts Best Low-Cap Crypto

Not much to bet on the first day. The direction only starts to lean after a week, and only slightly.

We measured BTC from the hourly candle of each 8:30 a.m. ET release on Binance, using the Bureau of Labor Statistics’ release archive for the dates. The October 2025 report, never published because of the government shutdown, is not included.

This year broke into two halves. Bitcoin was higher a week after each of 2026’s first seven jobs reports, by 0.5% to 7.6%. Then it fell after all three since August.

One caveat: a week leaves room for other news to take over. The worst 24 hours in the sample came after the December 2021 report, a 17.1% drop that came with a weekend leverage flush.

DISCOVER: Fed Meeting Today: Traders Price a 95% Hold and a 79% December Hike. What Does It Mean for Crypto?

Because the Fed is leaning toward a hike, not a cut. It held rates at 3.75% to 4% on October 28 in a 10–2 vote, and both dissenters wanted a quarter-point hike.

Thursday’s crash already cooled that bet. Kalshi’s odds of a December hike dropped 10 points in a day, while a hold rose from 22% to 32%.

A strong report would likely push hike odds back up. A weak one would give the Fed room to wait, and with it, one less reason for traders to sell risk.

DISCOVER: Bitcoin Holds $79,200 Before the Fed, Coinbase Earnings and a $10.9B Expiry: Which Way Next?

Not yet. BTC sits about $380 under its 200-day exponential moving average (EMA) at $76,540, a line it had held on every daily close since its August breakout.

The jobs report has rarely set Bitcoin’s direction on the day. For traders following Bitcoin news this week, the weekly close against the 200-day EMA will matter more than the payroll number.

Tesla is 'winding down' Cybertruck production at its Texas Gigafactory

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The factory space will be repurposed to accommodate the new Cybercab, Elon Musk said.

Tesla will no longer build the electric Cybertruck pickup at its Texas Gigafactory, Elon Musk confirmed on X. Production will wind down in the middle of 2027 to make room for increased production of the company's upcoming autonomous Cybercab instead. Unless the company moves manufacturing to another location, the stainless-steel truck could be near the end of its short but drama-filled existence.

Musk didn't say if production of the Cybertruck would be discontinued altogether. Given the vehicle's Ford Edsel-like sales and export issues, no one would be shocked if the James Bond and Blade Runner-inspired vehicle is relegated to history's scrap heap.

When it was first unveiled in November 2019, the Cybertruck acquired a lot of buzz for being like no other pickup ever seen. However, as with other Elon Musk promises, things quickly went trapezoid-shaped. Tesla never came close to delivering on the promised $39,900 price tag, with the cheapest version eventually priced at $59,900.

The Cybertruck also faced multiple recalls and quality control complaints, while its controversial design (though loved by some), was widely derided. As a result, Tesla has only sold around 71,000 Cybertrucks since deliveries started in late 2023, a far cry from the 250,000 per year the company originally projected.

In place of the Cybertruck, Tesla is allocating more capacity to the Cybercab autonomous robotaxi with no steering wheel and pedals. The success of that vehicle is also by no means guaranteed, given the current federal investigation into the vehicle and Tesla's lack of progress toward fully-autonomous self-driving after more than 10 years of trying.

Remember when Grand Theft Auto 6 was delayed, not once but twice? It's hard to forget: its first release date was treated as a day of mourning by some particularly upset fans. Despite all the outcries from fans, Take-Two Interactive chief Strauss Zelnick has repeatedly explained that he doesn't regret the decision.

Talking to WSJ the interviewer asks Zelnick what his immediate emotion was after hearing from the team that GTA 6 needed to be delayed: "I don't always feel calm but I tend to come across as calm … The timing of the call was not great for an array of reasons. We had just had a board retreat 10 days earlier so I wasn't thrilled about the timing. That said, this is a balancing act with all creative executives and it's not just time, it could be resources."

"These are very difficult decisions to make," Zelnick continues. "If, on the other hand, if as a refresher course, your mission is to make the best entertainment on earth and part of your three part strategy is to be the most creative, sometimes you have to make these expensive and inconvenient decisions in service of that mission goal.

The delay was essential, Zelnick says, to stay true to Take-Two's mission "to make the best entertainment on earth."

"What happens when people take the other approach," Zelnick asks. "The other approach being we're slavishly devoted to a schedule and we're just going to ship despite what is going on. I'm not going to throw any competitors under the bus, but if you know anything about the videogame business in the past few years there was a very high profile release that fell into this category. They shipped and they had to pull the title after shipping because it didn't work."

I don't think there's much doubt about what game Zelnick is referencing here, but just in case anyone is wondering, my bet would be on Concord—yeah, it's probably Concord. And for good reason: it didn't have a shred of the anticipation that GTA 6 has but was still bankrolled by a major studio and essentially burnt hundreds of millions of dollars—a cautionary tale if ever I heard one.

BYD’s electric hypercar hits 312 mph, first production car over 500 km/h

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

BYD’s Yangwang U9 Xtreme just reached 503.17 km/h (312.66 mph) on a closed highway in Abu Dhabi, making it the first production car ever to go faster than 500 km/h.

The FIA and Guinness World Records both recognized the run. It’s the all-electric hypercar’s second top-speed record in just over a year, and the one it broke was its own.

BYD announced the record on X this morning, saying the U9 Xtreme “has done the impossible” and calling it “the fastest production car on the planet.”

Abu Dhabi closed a 10 km section of the Sheikh Khalifa Bin Zayed International Road (E11), in the emirate’s Al Dhafra Region, for the attempt. That gave the team a 7 km straight. Marc Basseng, the German racing driver who set the car’s previous record, was behind the wheel again.

It took two days. Basseng maxed out at 490 km/h on the first day, per the FIA. After an overnight round of changes to the rear wing, damping, and tire pressures, he got to 503.17 km/h on his second attempt of November 1.

The previous mark was 496.22 km/h (308 mph), set at Germany’s ATP Papenburg proving ground in September 2025. That run had already pushed the Bugatti Chiron Super Sport 300+ and its 490.48 km/h from 2019 off the top of the list.

And it comes just days after BYD released footage of a U9 Xtreme crashing into a guardrail at about 280 mph during an earlier high-speed test. The driver walked away unharmed. BYD went right back out and hit 457.89 km/h on the next run.

The U9 Xtreme is the track-focused version of the Yangwang U9, the flagship supercar from BYD’s luxury brand. It uses four electric motors, each rated at 555 kW and spinning up to 30,000 rpm, for 2,220 kW combined. That’s over 3,000 PS, or nearly 3,000 hp.

It runs on a 1,200-volt architecture with a track-grade version of BYD’s Blade Battery. Those are LFP cells, the same chemistry BYD puts in its cheapest city cars.

For context, the Rimac Nevera R, which snagged a pile of EV world records last year, tops out at 431.45 km/h. The U9 Xtreme also holds the production EV lap record at the Nürburgring Nordschleife at 6:59.157.

Only 30 will be built, and BYD is charging around $3 million apiece. Don’t expect to see one in the US, where BYD doesn’t sell passenger cars.

The biggest one: this is a single-direction run. The two Guinness titles listed for the attempt, highest speed by a production car and by an electric production car, are both specified as “single direction.” BYD’s post claims four Guinness records, but the FIA’s announcement only lists those two for this run.

Production car top speed records have traditionally been the average of two runs in opposite directions, within an hour, to cancel out wind and road grade. That’s how Koenigsegg got its 447.19 km/h record with the Agera RS in 2017.

Bugatti took heat for exactly this in 2019, when the Chiron hit 490 km/h one way on a pre-production car. Bugatti itself said it wasn’t an official record.

The car also ran on bespoke semi-slick tires developed with Giti Tire. It’s not clear whether the 30 customer cars will get them.

A Chinese automaker best known for $10,000 city cars now builds the fastest production car in the world, and it’s electric. Not long ago, the conventional wisdom was that EVs couldn’t sustain high top speeds because the battery and motors would overheat long before the car ran out of road. BYD just ran 503 km/h on LFP cells. LFP cells!

But we’d hold BYD to the same standard we held Bugatti to. A one-way run on a highway with a 7 km straight is a great demo, not a definitive record, but impressive nonetheless.

Roadster 2 will now never be a record holder. It simple can't compete with today's hypercars nevermind those in 2028 when we actually see one leave a factory. Although with Tesla's record, it's usually 4 years from demo to production so that makes it 2030 for the Roadster 2.

And either way, let’s be honest: this is about marketing. Nobody is going to drive one of these 30 cars at 500 km/h. But it works. It’s a halo for Yangwang ahead of its new ultra-luxury sedan and for BYD’s push into Europe, and it makes the “Chinese cars are cheap copies” crowd look out of date.

Meanwhile, Tesla promised a Roadster that would hit over 250 mph back in 2017. Nine years later, it’s still not in production.

The real question now is whether Bugatti or Koenigsegg answers with a two-way 500 km/h run. Koenigsegg says its Jesko Absolut can theoretically do it, but it has never attempted a run. Is BYD what finally makes them try?

2028 Whitney Biennial to Be Curated by Meg Onli and Zack Winokur

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Whitney Museum has named the curators for the next Biennial: Whitney curator Meg Onli and director Zack Winokur. The 83rd edition of one of the country’s top biennials—and almost always most polarizing—will open in March 2028.

Onli will co-curate the Whitney Biennial for the second time, having done the 2024 edition, titled “Even Better Than the Real Thing,” with Chrissie Iles. Onli joined the Whitney as a curator-at-large in 2023, and she was promoted to the title of Nancy and Fred Poses Curator in 2024. She had previously been codirector and curator of the Underground Museum in Los Angeles and an associate curator at Institute of Contemporary Art, Philadelphia. Related Articles Whitney Museum Hires Nolan Jimbo as Associate Curator Roy Lichtenstein Saw Our Pictures-Obsessed Moment Coming--and Deliberately Fed Into It

With artist Alex Da Corte, Onli is also a co-curator of the Whitney’s current retrospective for Pop artist Roy Lichtenstein, which opened last month. In a review, ARTnews deputy editor Alex Greenberger called it a “stylish show” that contends that Lichtenstein “saw our pictures-obsessed moment coming … and even deliberately fed into it.”

Onli’s collaboration with Da Corte for an exhibition is perhaps an indicator of what visitors can likely expect in her working with Winokur when the Biennial opens in less than a year and a half. A press release announcing their appointment described Onli and Winokur as bringing “expansive perspectives on artistic practice today and a shared commitment to championing artists, fostering experimentation, and exploring new possibilities across disciplines.”

“I’m thrilled to be returning to the Whitney Biennial,” Onli said in a statement. “From the Whitney’s early Annuals to its many subsequent iterations, the Biennial has a rich history of reinvention that is already giving us much to think about. I couldn’t be more excited to explore what the Biennial can be alongside Zack, who is such a dynamic thinker, and to see where our conversations with artists lead.”

Winokur is the cofounder of AMOC* (American Modern Opera Company), which presented a five-week festival at Lincoln Center in 2025 that included 12 new productions. He has directed productions like Complications in Sue and The Black Clown at the Opera Philadelphia, as well as Both Ends, starring Justin Vivian Bond and made to celebrate a partnership with A24 and Dior. He also has collaborated on an installation for the Cooper Hewitt Smithsonian Design Triennial with artists Hugh Hayden and Davóne Tines.

He is also the former artistic director of Little Island, which was an off-site venue of the 2026 Whitney Biennial, which closed less than a month ago and saw more than 550,000 visitors. That edition, curated by Marcela Guerrero and Drew Sawyer, saw Julio Torres and Martine Gutierrez stage a commissioned performance, titled Marina, on Little Island this past August.

In a statement about his working with Onli, Winokur said, “Our shared desire to center objects and people and the collision between them already echoes our collaboration. It’s that energy that makes the dialogue with her so fun and inspiring. As an artist, what excites me most is being in service to other artists, and I’m delighted to have the opportunity to do so in the context of the Whitney.”

Speaking of the “distinct curatorial alchemy” of each Biennial and the collaboration between Onli and Winokur specifically, Whitney director Scott Rothkopf said in a statement, “Meg is one of the most compelling curatorial voices working today, with an extraordinary ability to champion artists and bring fresh perspectives to the history of American art. Zack is an equally inventive artistic programmer who has become one of the leading voices of his generation through groundbreaking work across opera, theater, dance, and the visual arts. Their partnership promises a radical new vision for the 2028 Biennial.”

Lockheed Built The Constellation’s Famous Triple Tail For One Key Reason - To Fit Inside An Airline’s Existing Hangars

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Brandon's passion for aviation started at a young age. He became involved in the flight simulator and VATSIM communities, which sparked his interest in aviation and eventually led him to earn his private pilot license. Outside of work, he continues to build flight hours, explore new airports, and stay involved in the aviation community.

The Lockheed Constellation is one of the most recognizable aircraft ever built, and its most distinctive feature, the triple tail, was not an aesthetic choice. It was a solution to a hangar door. When TWA asked Lockheed to design a long-range pressurized airliner in 1939, one of the requirements was that the aircraft had to fit inside TWA's existing maintenance facilities. A single vertical stabilizer large enough to provide directional stability on a four-engine aircraft of the Constellation's size and speed would have made the tail too tall to clear the hangar doors.

Lockheed's engineers split the required fin area across three shorter vertical stabilizers, keeping the aircraft's overall height to 23 feet 8 inches (7.2 meters). The solution worked; TWA got an aircraft that fit its infrastructure, and Lockheed produced 856 Constellations across all variants before the jet age ended the propeller airliner era. Here is why the triple tail was necessary, how it worked aerodynamically, and what the aircraft contributed to commercial aviation beyond its silhouette.

In 1939, Trans World Airlines owner Howard Hughes approached Lockheed with a set of requirements for a new long-range airliner. Hughes wanted an aircraft that could carry a 6,000-lb (2,720 kg) payload, fly 3,500 miles (5,630 km) without payload, and cruise at more than 250 mph (402 km/h) at 20,000 feet (6,096 meters). Those were aggressive specifications for the era and would have produced a large, heavy, four-engine aircraft substantially bigger than anything TWA was operating. Hughes added one additional requirement: the aircraft had to fit inside TWA's existing maintenance hangars.

That constraint created a specific engineering problem. The Constellation's four Wright R-3350 engines drove large-diameter propellers that required tall landing gear to maintain ground clearance. The tall gear raised the fuselage, which in turn raised the tail. A conventional single vertical stabilizer providing enough area for directional stability on an aircraft of this size and speed would have pushed the total height well beyond what TWA's hangar doors could accommodate. Lockheed's engineers, led by chief engineer Hall Hibbard and chief aerodynamicist Kelly Johnson, needed to provide the required vertical stabilizer area without exceeding the hangar height limit.

The solution was to split the vertical stabilizer into three shorter fins mounted across the horizontal tailplane. Three fins provided the same total surface area as a single large fin while keeping each individual surface short enough to hold the aircraft's overall height to 23 feet 8 inches (7.2 meters). The design fit inside TWA's hangars.

The aerodynamic function of a vertical stabilizer is to provide directional stability by resisting unwanted yaw, keeping the tail of the aircraft tracking behind the nose in straight flight and during turns. The size of the fin determines how much yaw resistance it generates. A larger fin produces more stability. The total area required depends on the aircraft's size, weight, speed range, and the distance between the center of gravity and the tail surfaces. On the Constellation, splitting that area across three fins rather than concentrating it in one did not reduce the total stabilizer area. It redistributed it across three shorter surfaces.

The three-fin arrangement also produced a secondary aerodynamic benefit that a single center fin would not have provided. The two outboard fins sit in the slipstream of the inboard propellers, which means they receive accelerated airflow from the engines at all flight speeds. At low speeds, where a conventional vertical stabilizer is least effective because the airflow over it is slowest, the propeller wash over the outboard fins maintains a higher local airspeed across those surfaces. The result is improved directional stability during takeoff, landing, and low-speed flight, and better yaw authority during single-engine operations when the aircraft needs to counteract the asymmetric thrust of three operating engines against one failed engine.

The triple-tail configuration came with trade-offs. The horizontal stabilizer had to be structurally reinforced to support three fins and the associated loads, which added weight. The flight control system required three rudders instead of one, with corresponding control cables, hinges, and actuators that added mechanical complexity and maintenance requirements. The Douglas DC-6, which competed directly with the Constellation on the same routes, used a conventional single-fin tail and did not pay those weight and complexity penalties.

A nostalgic journey awaits as Lufthansa's iconic aircraft nears completion.

The Constellation was designed at Lockheed's Burbank, California facility under the leadership of two engineers whose careers extended well beyond the program. Hall Hibbard served as chief engineer and Kelly Johnson as chief aerodynamicist during the initial design phase, with project engineer Don Palmer managing the detailed engineering as the program progressed. Johnson, who was 29 when the Constellation project began, would later found Lockheed's Advanced Development Programs division, better known as Skunk Works, where he led the design of the P-80, U-2, and SR-71.

The Constellation's wing was derived from the Lockheed P-38 Lightning fighter, scaled up from a 52-foot (15.8 meters) span to 123 feet (37.5 meters). The structural approach and airfoil design carried over from the fighter program, which gave Lockheed's team a tested aerodynamic foundation to build on rather than starting from a clean sheet. The fuselage was a pressurized aluminum semi-monocoque structure with the distinctive curved dolphin shape that rose at the nose, dipped at the midsection, and rose again toward the tail. The pressurization system maintained a cabin altitude of approximately 8,000 feet (2,438 meters) while cruising at 20,000 feet (6,096 meters), making it one of the first large commercial airliners to offer pressurized flight as a standard feature.

Power came from four Wright R-3350 Duplex-Cyclone 18-cylinder radial engines, the same engine family that powered the Boeing B-29 Superfortress. Each engine produced approximately 2,200 horsepower in early variants, later increased to 2,700 and beyond in the Super Constellation series. The R-3350 was powerful but temperamental, prone to overheating issues that contributed to several incidents during the Constellation's early service years. The aircraft also introduced hydraulically boosted flight controls and a thermal deicing system on the wing and tail leading edges, features that were not standard on contemporary airliners.

The Constellation first flew on January 9, 1943, from Lockheed's Burbank field. It flew not as a TWA airliner but as a military transport designated C-69, requisitioned by the US Army Air Forces after the attack on Pearl Harbor redirected Lockheed's production priorities toward the war effort. The nine L-049 airframes originally ordered by TWA were purchased by the War Department, and 22 C-69s were built between 1943 and 1945. The aircraft spent the war years stateside, used primarily for long-range personnel transport while the Douglas C-54 Skymaster handled the higher-priority overseas airlift missions.

On April 17, 1944, Howard Hughes and TWA president Jack Frye flew the second production C-69 from Burbank to Washington National Airport in 6 hours and 57 minutes, averaging 331 mph (533 km/h) at 65% engine power. The aircraft was painted in full TWA livery despite carrying a military serial number, and the flight was a publicity exercise as much as a delivery. Hughes was required to hand the aircraft over to the Army Air Forces upon landing in Washington. On the return trip, the aircraft stopped at Wright Field in Ohio, where Orville Wright boarded for what would be his last flight, more than 40 years after his first at Kitty Hawk. Wright commented that the Constellation's wingspan was greater than the distance of that first flight.

When the war ended, TWA bought back every C-69 it could from the government and converted them to civilian configuration. Commercial Constellation service began in February 1946, and the aircraft immediately outperformed the Douglas DC-4, the airliner most carriers were using on long-haul domestic and early transatlantic routes. The DC-4 was unpressurized and cruised at approximately 227 mph (365 km/h). The pressurized Constellation cruised at 340 mph (547 km/h) at 20,000 feet (6,096 meters), above most weather. Douglas responded with the pressurized DC-6 in 1947, and later the DC-7, and the resulting competition between Lockheed and Douglas through the 1950s produced the most intensive period of propeller airliner development in aviation history. Lockheed built 856 Constellations across all variants before production ended in 1958, the same year the Boeing 707 entered service and rendered the entire category obsolete.

From the TriStar Stargazer to Buffalo’s Electras and Australia’s Connie — meet the oldest Lockheed commercial aircraft still flying today.

The Constellation introduced several technologies that became standard across commercial aviation. Cabin pressurization at the scale Lockheed implemented allowed airline passengers to fly above weather for the first time on a production airliner, establishing the principle that commercial aircraft should cruise at altitudes where turbulence and storms are below rather than around the aircraft.

Hydraulically boosted flight controls addressed the increasing force required to move control surfaces on larger, faster aircraft, a problem that would only grow as airliners became heavier through the 1950s and into the jet age. The thermal deicing system on wing and tail leading edges replaced the rubber boot deicing used on earlier aircraft with a more reliable heated-air approach. Triple tails disappeared from commercial aviation when jet airliners arrived.

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

The Boeing 707, Douglas DC-8, and every subsequent commercial jet used a single vertical stabilizer. The hangars that had constrained the Constellation's designers were replaced or expanded to accommodate the new generation of aircraft, removing the height limitation that had created the three-fin design in the first place. By the time airports were building hangars for the 747 in the late 1960s, no vertical stabilizer height was too tall to accommodate. The engineering problem the triple tail solved ceased to exist.

Of the 856 Constellations built between 1943 and 1958, approximately 55 survive. As of 2026, only two are airworthy. VH-EAG, a C-121C Super Constellation operated by Australia's Historical Aircraft Restoration Society, flies from Illawarra Regional Airport in a restored Qantas livery and carries passengers on demonstration flights. N422NA, a VC-121A named Bataan that served as General Douglas MacArthur's personal transport and later flew for NASA during the Apollo program, is maintained by the Air Legends Foundation and appears at US airshows.

Elon Musk’s Tesla and SpaceX Join 67 Firms Rejecting OKXICE Tokenised Stocks

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Elon Musk’s Tesla and SpaceX have joined a growing list of companies rejecting OKXICE tokenized stocks trading platform, bringing the total objections to 67. The update added Palantir, S&P Global, KLA, Super Micro Computer and Meta to the list. That puts 56 of the 62 companies originally selected for the platform among those objecting to their shares being traded through the proposed venue.

OKX–ICE Stock Venue’s Issuer Objections Reach 62Boeing and ServiceNow have raised objections to the venture’s proposed symbols, leaving eight of the 63 originally proposed symbols without a disclosed opt-out.⚠️ Personal market analysis only. NFA — manage risk and DYOR.… pic.twitter.com/CXjQRpQOnH

The development comes as Musk recently attracted attention for responding to a separate ICE-backed Hyperliquid initiative. The billionaire replied “Interesting” to investor Stanley Druckenmiller’s October 29 announcement about investing in Trade[XYZ], which is partnering with ICE to bring Hyperliquid-based perpetual futures to US markets.

The growing opposition presents a significant obstacle for OKXICE tokenized stocks plan, a joint venture between cryptocurrency exchange OKX and Intercontinental Exchange, the parent company of the New York Stock Exchange.

The proposed platform aims to bring tokenised versions of US stocks onto blockchain infrastructure, allowing investors to trade around the clock using stablecoins such as USDC. But companies have increasingly exercised their right to prevent unaffiliated platforms from offering tokenised versions of their shares.

Tesla and SpaceX appeared in OKX-ICE’s November 2 notice alongside Amazon, Netflix, Lam Research and SoFi Technologies. Subsequent updates pushed the total number of objecting companies to 67 by today.

The original selection included 62 companies covering 63 proposed stock tickers. The latest tally therefore extends beyond the initial roster, with 56 original companies having filed objections.

Under the SEC’s temporary Innovation Exemption, trading venues must notify companies before offering third-party tokenised versions of their shares. Issuers can object within 30 days, preventing the venue from listing those particular tokenised shares.

Importantly, these objections do not represent a blanket rejection of tokenisation. Companies can still explore their own tokenisation arrangements or participate through other venues.

OKXICE tokenized stocks proposal uses permissioned liquidity pools on OKX’s X Layer blockchain, with Uniswap v4 infrastructure supporting trading. Investors would undergo identity and sanctions checks before accessing the platform.

The initiative aims to combine blockchain settlement with extended trading hours, although the growing number of objections raises questions about which major stocks could eventually become available.

Discover: The Crypto Presales This Q4 2026

The growing resistance involving Musk’s companies creates an interesting contrast with his recent interaction with the Hyperliquid ecosystem. On October 29, billionaire investor Stanley Druckenmiller announced his investment in Trade[XYZ], revealing plans to use its products following a partnership with ICE.

Musk responded to Druckenmiller’s post on X with a single word: “Interesting.”

The announcement involved a separate ICE initiative designed to bring perpetual futures trading to the United States using Hyperliquid infrastructure. Under the proposed arrangement, ICE will operate the regulated exchange and clearing infrastructure, while Trade[XYZ] will develop the markets using Hyperliquid’s technology.

The platform intends to offer around-the-clock perpetual contracts linked to cryptocurrencies, equities, commodities, and foreign exchange. ICE is also making a strategic investment in Trade[XYZ], giving the agreement a direct institutional connection to the growing market for blockchain-based financial products.
